    *   **Dependency on Tokenizer:** Tokenization is required to calculate the conditional probabilities. Any issues in the tokenization used will be amplified when training with PCPO, potentially leading to suboptimal results. This aspect can be dependent on the architecture used.
    *   **Need for Gold Answers:** The algorithm still relies on the availability of "gold" answers to create the candidate pairs, reducing its suitability for some use cases. Future work towards making the algorithm work without gold answers will improve its applicability.

*   **Potential Influence:** PCPO has the potential to influence future research in preference optimization, reward modeling, and LLM training by highlighting the importance of internal coherence. It provides a new avenue for improving the reasoning capabilities of LLMs beyond simple outcome-based criteria. Other researchers could build on PCPO by exploring different ways of measuring and incorporating internal consistency, potentially leading to even more effective methods.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:** PCPO presents a significant advancement in preference optimization by explicitly modeling and incorporating token-level consistency. The consistent experimental results, clear methodology, and valuable analysis support this evaluation. However, the increased computational cost and dependency on gold labels limit the method's immediate impact to some extent. Despite these limitations, the paper demonstrates a valuable contribution to the field and has the potential to stimulate further research in improving the reasoning capabilities of LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Position Paper: Metadata Enrichment Model: Integrating Neural Networks and Semantic Knowledge Graphs for Cultural Heritage Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23543v1)
- **Authors**: Jan Ignatowicz, Krzysztof Kutt, Grzegorz J. Nalepa
- **Abstract**: The digitization of cultural heritage collections has opened new directions for research, yet the lack of enriched metadata poses a substantial challenge to accessibility, interoperability, and cross-institutional collaboration. In several past years neural networks models such as YOLOv11 and Detectron2 have revolutionized visual data analysis, but their application to domain-specific cultural artifacts - such as manuscripts and incunabula - remains limited by the absence of methodologies that address structural feature extraction and semantic interoperability. In this position paper, we argue, that the integration of neural networks with semantic technologies represents a paradigm shift in cultural heritage digitization processes. We present the Metadata Enrichment Model (MEM), a conceptual framework designed to enrich metadata for digitized collections by combining fine-tuned computer vision models, large language models (LLMs) and structured knowledge graphs. The Multilayer Vision Mechanism (MVM) appears as the key innovation of MEM. This iterative process improves visual analysis by dynamically detecting nested features, such as text within seals or images within stamps. To expose MEM's potential, we apply it to a dataset of digitized incunabula from the Jagiellonian Digital Library and release a manually annotated dataset of 105 manuscript pages. We examine the practical challenges of MEM's usage in real-world GLAM institutions, including the need for domain-specific fine-tuning, the adjustment of enriched metadata with Linked Data standards and computational costs. We present MEM as a flexible and extensible methodology. This paper contributes to the discussion on how artificial intelligence and semantic web technologies can advance cultural heritage research, and also use these technologies in practice.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Metadata Enrichment Model (MEM), a framework for improving metadata for digitized cultural heritage collections. MEM combines computer vision models (e.g., YOLO, Detectron2), large language models (LLMs), and semantic knowledge graphs to automate metadata enrichment.  A key aspect is the Multilayer Vision Mechanism (MVM), which iteratively analyzes images to uncover nested features, such as text within seals. The framework enriches metadata and connects them to external databases using knowledge graphs. The paper includes a case study with digitized incunabula, a manually annotated dataset of 105 pages, and discusses challenges like domain-specific fine-tuning and computational costs.  The authors position MEM as a flexible and extensible methodology for advancing cultural heritage research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The novelty lies in the *integration* of existing technologies (CV, LLMs, knowledge graphs) into a cohesive framework specifically designed for the nuances of cultural heritage digitization. While each component (YOLO, LLMs, knowledge graphs) is individually well-established, their *synergistic application* with iterative visual analysis (MVM) to address the specific challenges of complex cultural artifacts seems to be where the primary novelty resides. The creation and release of a domain-specific annotated dataset is also a valuable contribution, helping facilitate further research in this area.  However, the idea of using LLMs to enrich metadata is not entirely new. The paper differentiates itself by focusing on the *structured and iterative* manner in which the LLMs are integrated, rather than simply applying them as a black box for metadata generation.

* **Significance:** The significance comes from addressing a practical and persistent problem: the lack of enriched, structured metadata in digital heritage collections. The framework has the potential to improve accessibility, interoperability, and cross-institutional collaboration. By automating the enrichment process, it reduces the reliance on manual annotation, which is a significant bottleneck. The explicit focus on linking enriched metadata to Linked Data standards is also important for ensuring long-term interoperability and integration with the broader semantic web. The paper's focus on nested features (seals, etc.) is highly relevant for a range of heritage materials and moves beyond simple object detection. By addressing the ethical concerns related to algorithmic bias and cultural representation, it strengthens its significance to the overall digitization effort.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addresses a real-world problem:** The lack of good metadata is a major pain point in cultural heritage digitization.
    * **Integrative approach:**  Combining different technologies is a promising approach.
    * **Multilayer Vision Mechanism (MVM):** Iterative analysis for nested features is a key strength.
    * **Focus on semantic interoperability:**  Linking to Linked Data standards ensures long-term value.
    * **Practical evaluation:**  The case study and annotated dataset provide concrete evidence of the framework's utility.
    * **Discussion of ethical considerations:** Acknowledging algorithmic bias and cultural representation shows a responsible approach.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited evaluation:** The case study, while useful, is based on a relatively small dataset (105 pages). More extensive evaluation is needed to demonstrate scalability and generalizability.
    * **Computational demands:** The paper mentions computational costs as a potential limitation, but doesn't quantify them. This information is crucial for GLAM institutions with limited resources.
    * **Ontology development:** While the paper discusses dynamic ontology expansion, the process of aligning domain-specific terms with external databases (e.g., Wikidata) needs to be elaborated more.
    * **Reliance on existing tools:** The framework heavily relies on the performance of existing CV and LLM models. Improvements in these models will directly benefit MEM, but also means that MEM's success is dependent on external factors.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    * Providing a concrete framework for automated metadata enrichment.
    * Encouraging further research on integrating AI and semantic technologies for cultural heritage.
    * Highlighting the importance of iterative visual analysis for complex artifacts.
    * Promoting the use of Linked Data standards in digital heritage.
    * Raising awareness of ethical considerations in AI-driven cultural heritage applications.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a promising and practical framework (MEM) that effectively integrates established technologies to address a crucial problem in cultural heritage digitization: the lack of rich and structured metadata. The paper presents a novel and useful technique (MVM) for automated metadata generation that has the potential to improve interoperability of different GLAM institutions.

However, the evaluation is limited by the dataset size, and computational costs are not fully quantified. The framework's reliance on existing tools is both a strength and a weakness. Also, the paper doesn't present a deep dive into ontology development. The technical evaluation remains basic, and there is a need to provide comprehensive tests on general use case to strengthen it.

While some components are not entirely new, the integration and adaptation to the specific challenges of cultural heritage represent a significant contribution. Therefore, a score of 7 reflects the paper's novelty, significance, and potential influence, balanced with the limitations in evaluation and implementation details.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Translation in the Wild
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23548v1)
- **Authors**: Yuri Balashov
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) excel in translation among other things, demonstrating competitive performance for many language pairs in zero- and few-shot settings. But unlike dedicated neural machine translation models, LLMs are not trained on any translation-related objective. What explains their remarkable translation abilities? Are these abilities grounded in "incidental bilingualism" (Briakou et al. 2023) in training data? Does instruction tuning contribute to it? Are LLMs capable of aligning and leveraging semantically identical or similar monolingual contents from different corners of the internet that are unlikely to fit in a single context window? I offer some reflections on this topic, informed by recent studies and growing user experience. My working hypothesis is that LLMs' translation abilities originate in two different types of pre-training data that may be internalized by the models in different ways. I discuss the prospects for testing the "duality" hypothesis empirically and its implications for reconceptualizing translation, human and machine, in the age of deep learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the translation capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  It argues that LLMs' translation abilities arise from a duality:  *Local learning*, derived from incidental bilingual data within a single context window, and *Global learning*,  which leverages semantically related monolingual content spread across the vast pre-training dataset. The paper contrasts LLMs with traditional Neural Machine Translation (NMT) models, highlighting differences in architecture, training data, objectives, and error patterns. It reviews recent research on LLMs in translation, discussing zero-shot, few-shot, pivoting, and chain-of-thought prompting.  It explores the inner workings of LLMs during translation, examining language-agnostic representations and how models navigate between languages. The paper proposes several empirical tests to validate the duality hypothesis, including data ablation studies, synthetic tasks, and human evaluation, and discusses the implications for our understanding of translation in the age of deep learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly Articulated Hypothesis:**  The duality hypothesis is well-defined and provides a coherent framework for understanding LLM translation.
    *   **Comprehensive Review:**  The paper synthesizes a vast amount of recent literature on LLMs and translation, creating a valuable overview of the field.
    *   **Thought-Provoking Analysis:** The paper raises important conceptual questions about the nature of translation and the role of LLMs in the process. The comparison with human translation and the emphasis on pluralism are insightful.
    *   **Empirical Predictions:**  The paper offers concrete empirical predictions that can be tested, paving the way for further research. The suggestions for experimental designs are valuable.
    *   **Addresses a Fundamental Question:** The paper tackles a central question in the current AI landscape: How do LLMs perform tasks they were not explicitly trained for?

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Hypothetical Nature:** The core argument, while compelling, remains largely hypothetical. The evidence presented is circumstantial, relying on interpretations of existing studies and anecdotal observations.
    *   **Lack of Novel Experiments:** The paper does not present new experimental results to directly support the duality hypothesis. It mainly proposes experiments for future research.
    *   **Oversimplification?:** The Local/Global distinction, while useful, could be an oversimplification of complex learning dynamics within LLMs.
    *   **Limited Depth of Mechanistic Understanding:** While the paper discusses recent interpretability work, it acknowledges the limited understanding of the inner workings of LLMs.
    *   **Uneven Rigor in Evidence:** The reliance on anecdotal evidence and interpretations from varied studies, without a systematic, controlled investigation, dilutes the strength of the argument.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The duality hypothesis itself isn't entirely groundbreaking. The idea that LLMs learn from different sources (both explicit and implicit) has been hinted at in other works. However, this paper is one of the first to rigorously articulate and frame it as a duality between local and global learning in the specific context of machine translation, providing a more nuanced understanding of the interplay between these two sources.
    *   The paper's strength lies in its attempt to reconcile diverse findings within a cohesive framework. It provides a valuable perspective on existing literature.
    *   The paper encourages a more critical examination of LLM training data and a move away from purely black-box approaches.
    *   The paper's theoretical implications for translation studies (e.g., pluralism, competence) are potentially significant but need to be further explored.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could stimulate new research directions focused on understanding the different learning mechanisms within LLMs.
    *   It could influence the development of new evaluation methods for LLM translation, focusing on fluency vs. fidelity and identifying specific error patterns.
    *   It could inform the design of more effective training strategies for LLMs, taking into account the importance of both local and global learning.
    *   It can help improve how we build and trust future translation systems.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses, the paper makes a noteworthy, but not revolutionary contribution.  It is a well-reasoned theoretical paper that synthesizes existing knowledge, proposes a compelling hypothesis, and offers valuable directions for future research. However, the lack of novel experimental results to support the hypothesis and a reliance on some anecdotal evidence limits its immediate impact.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### LLM-based Property-based Test Generation for Guardrailing Cyber-Physical Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23549v1)
- **Authors**: Khashayar Etemadi, Marjan Sirjani, Mahshid Helali Moghadam, Per Strandberg, Paul Pettersson
- **Abstract**: Cyber-physical systems (CPSs) are complex systems that integrate physical, computational, and communication subsystems. The heterogeneous nature of these systems makes their safety assurance challenging. In this paper, we propose a novel automated approach for guardrailing cyber-physical systems using property-based tests (PBTs) generated by Large Language Models (LLMs). Our approach employs an LLM to extract properties from the code and documentation of CPSs. Next, we use the LLM to generate PBTs that verify the extracted properties on the CPS. The generated PBTs have two uses. First, they are used to test the CPS before it is deployed, i.e., at design time. Secondly, these PBTs can be used after deployment, i.e., at run time, to monitor the behavior of the system and guardrail it against unsafe states. We implement our approach in ChekProp and conduct preliminary experiments to evaluate the generated PBTs in terms of their relevance (how well they match manually crafted properties), executability (how many run with minimal manual modification), and effectiveness (coverage of the input space partitions). The results of our experiments and evaluation demonstrate a promising path forward for creating guardrails for CPSs using LLM-generated property-based tests.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents CHEKPROP, a novel approach for guardrailing cyber-physical systems (CPSs) using property-based tests (PBTs) generated by large language models (LLMs). CHEKPROP extracts properties from CPS code, documentation, and unit tests using an LLM. It then uses the LLM to generate PBTs to verify these properties, both at design time (for pre-deployment testing) and at runtime (for continuous monitoring and safety enforcement). A prototype of CHEKPROP is implemented and evaluated on several CPS programs, showing promising results in terms of relevance, executability, and effectiveness of the generated PBTs.  The experiments compare the LLM-extracted properties against manually-defined properties and asses the quality of the generated tests based on how easily they can be run, and whether they cover a large range of inputs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs for PBT generation is not entirely new, with prior work exploring this for standard software APIs. However, the application of LLMs to generate PBTs *specifically for guardrailing CPSs* and considering the unique challenges of CPS (interaction of the physical and cyber parts, environmental mocking) demonstrates a moderate level of novelty. The inclusion of the continuous run-time property verification aspect also adds to the novelty factor. The contribution over existing literature, particularly [18], is in extending the scope to CPS by accounting for reasoning about sensors, actuators and continuous dynamics.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in addressing the critical safety challenges in CPS. Automating the generation of PBTs, a task that typically requires considerable manual effort and domain expertise, has the potential to significantly improve the safety and reliability of CPS. The ability to extract properties *and* create PBTs for CPS, tested at design time, then translated into guardrail rules at runtime, is an important step towards creating more robust CPS.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The problem is well-motivated.
    *   The approach is clearly presented and well-structured.
    *   The experiments, while preliminary, provide valuable insights into the effectiveness of CHEKPROP. The evaluation metrics (relevance, executability, effectiveness) are appropriate.
    *   The paper identifies key challenges in applying LLMs to CPS testing (e.g., complexities of mocking CPS environments, limited training data).
    *   The open-source availability of CHEKPROP [4] supports reproducibility and further research.
    *   The experiments provide valuable insight and actionable advice for practitioners about how to make LLMs more effective in this context.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation is limited in scale.  Testing on a larger and more diverse set of CPS examples would strengthen the findings significantly.
    *   The PBT improvement loop is currently disabled, which represents a missed opportunity to further refine and improve the generated tests.  The current evaluation only captures the "initial" LLM generated PBT and not its iterative improvements.
    *   The paper lacks detailed consideration of the computational overhead associated with running LLMs for runtime property verification. This could be a limitation for resource-constrained CPS.
    *  The comparison focuses on only two categories of related work. The inclusion of research on search-based software testing, and test amplification techniques could have enriched the discussion and provided more robust comparisons.

*   **Potential Influence:** CHEKPROP has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   Inspiring further research into the use of LLMs for CPS testing and verification.
    *   Providing a practical tool that engineers can use to automate PBT generation for their CPS.
    *   Raising awareness of the unique challenges and opportunities in applying LLMs to CPS.
    *  The approach addresses a need in the community for techniques capable of efficiently generating meaningful test cases for systems with complicated, interdependent constraints.

*   **Justification of Score:**
    The paper presents a moderately novel application of LLMs to address a critical problem in CPS safety. While the underlying techniques are not entirely groundbreaking, the CPS focus and end-to-end integration (property generation, PBT generation, runtime verification) makes it a significant contribution. The experimental results are promising, but require further validation with more extensive case studies and further investigation is warranted into the PBT improvement loop.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Sustainable Carbon-Aware and Water-Efficient LLM Scheduling in Geo-Distributed Cloud Datacenters
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23554v1)
- **Authors**: Hayden Moore, Sirui Qi, Ninad Hogade, Dejan Milojicic, Cullen Bash, Sudeep Pasricha
- **Abstract**: In recent years, Large Language Models (LLM) such as ChatGPT, CoPilot, and Gemini have been widely adopted in different areas. As the use of LLMs continues to grow, many efforts have focused on reducing the massive training overheads of these models. But it is the environmental impact of handling user requests to LLMs that is increasingly becoming a concern. Recent studies estimate that the costs of operating LLMs in their inference phase can exceed training costs by 25x per year. As LLMs are queried incessantly, the cumulative carbon footprint for the operational phase has been shown to far exceed the footprint during the training phase. Further, estimates indicate that 500 ml of fresh water is expended for every 20-50 requests to LLMs during inference. To address these important sustainability issues with LLMs, we propose a novel framework called SLIT to co-optimize LLM quality of service (time-to-first token), carbon emissions, water usage, and energy costs. The framework utilizes a machine learning (ML) based metaheuristic to enhance the sustainability of LLM hosting across geo-distributed cloud datacenters. Such a framework will become increasingly vital as LLMs proliferate.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the growing environmental concerns associated with LLM inference, specifically carbon emissions, water usage, and energy costs. It proposes a novel framework called SLIT (Sustainable LLM Inference Task scheduling) to co-optimize these factors along with the time-to-first-token (TTFT) performance metric. SLIT utilizes a metaheuristic approach combining machine learning (ML), specifically gradient boosting, and an evolutionary algorithm (EA) to generate a Pareto optimal solution set for LLM request scheduling across geo-distributed cloud datacenters. The framework models carbon footprint, water usage, and energy costs, and compares against existing scheduling methods. The results show that SLIT can significantly reduce carbon emissions, water usage, and energy costs while maintaining competitive TTFT performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its holistic approach to LLM inference scheduling. While previous works have addressed individual aspects like energy efficiency or carbon emissions, SLIT uniquely integrates carbon, water, energy, and TTFT considerations into a single framework. The combination of gradient boosting and an evolutionary algorithm within the metaheuristic to generate a Pareto front is also a valuable contribution. The application of these optimization techniques in the specific context of LLM inference within geo-distributed datacenters is also relatively new.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the increasing importance of sustainable LLM deployment. The operational costs and environmental impacts of LLMs, particularly inference, are becoming major concerns. SLIT offers a practical approach to mitigate these concerns by enabling datacenter managers to make informed scheduling decisions based on a range of objectives. The framework provides flexibility in balancing performance, cost, and environmental impact, making it adaptable to different operational needs and priorities.  The findings demonstrate substantial reductions in resource consumption while retaining acceptable latency, underscoring the potential impact of the proposed method. The use of a real-world LLM trace adds credibility to the evaluation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper comprehensively models the various components contributing to the environmental impact and performance of LLM inference.
    *   The proposed framework, SLIT, is well-designed, integrating ML and EA techniques for effective optimization.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of SLIT in achieving significant reductions in carbon emissions, water usage, and energy costs.
    *   The comparison against state-of-the-art scheduling approaches highlights the advantages of SLIT.
    *   The paper's clear structure, detailed descriptions, and well-presented figures enhance readability and understanding.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The evaluation relies on a synthetic workload derived from a real LLM trace. While this is a reasonable approach, a direct evaluation with actual LLM workloads would be more compelling. Although the synthetic trace does provide good general insight, the limitations of this type of synthetic evaluation should be explicitly stated.
    *   The hyperparameter tuning for the ML and EA components is not explicitly discussed. The sensitivity of the framework's performance to these hyperparameters could be further explored.
    *   The scalability of SLIT to even larger datacenters or more complex network topologies could be further investigated.
    *   A deeper discussion on the computational overhead of the metaheuristic scheduling process itself would be valuable. The maximum runtime is capped at 15 minutes, so this is potentially a major limitation that should be highlighted more clearly.
    *   The model doesn't consider the impact of geographical events on data center operations. While not essential, it could be a point for future work.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on sustainable LLM deployment. It could encourage further investigation into multi-objective optimization techniques for LLM scheduling and stimulate the development of new frameworks that integrate environmental considerations. The proposed models for carbon footprint, water usage, and energy costs could serve as a valuable resource for other researchers in the field.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** While the paper demonstrates a significant contribution by addressing the environmental costs associated with LLM inference and presents a viable framework for optimizing multiple, often conflicting, objectives, it falls slightly short of a higher score due to the points discussed in the weaknesses, particularly the reliance on a synthetic workload, hyperparameter tuning details, and lack of computational overhead investigation. The framework's computational runtime of 15 minutes is also a significant limitation which must be considered. Also, although multi-objective optimization in datacenters is not new, its application in the context of geo-distributed LLM inference is not as commonly studied. Thus, even though SLIT provides significant gains in multi-objective criteria, its novelty is only a modest enhancement, but not a breakthrough, compared to current studies. The limitations should be stated more clearly in the paper.

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Adaptive Federated LoRA in Heterogeneous Wireless Networks with Independent Sampling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23555v1)
- **Authors**: Yanzhao Hou, Jiaxiang Geng, Boyu Li, Xiaofeng Tao, Juncheng Wang, Xiaodong Xu, Bing Luo
- **Abstract**: Federated LoRA has emerged as a promising technique for efficiently fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on distributed devices by reducing the number of trainable parameters. However, existing approaches often inadequately overlook the theoretical and practical implications of system and data heterogeneity, thereby failing to optimize the overall training efficiency, particularly in terms of wall-clock time. In this paper, we propose an adaptive federated LoRA strategy with independent client sampling to minimize the convergence wall-clock time of federated fine-tuning under both computation and communication heterogeneity. We first derive a new convergence bound for federated LoRA with arbitrary and independent client sampling, notably without requiring the stringent bounded gradient assumption. Then, we introduce an adaptive bandwidth allocation scheme that accounts for heterogeneous client resources and system bandwidth constraints. Based on the derived theory, we formulate and solve a non-convex optimization problem to jointly determine the LoRA sketching ratios and sampling probabilities, aiming to minimize wall-clock convergence time. An efficient and low-complexity algorithm is developed to approximate the solution. Finally, extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach significantly reduces wall-clock training time compared to state-of-the-art methods across various models and datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper tackles the challenge of efficiently fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) in a federated learning (FL) setting, specifically focusing on the Federated LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) approach. It addresses the limitations of existing Federated LoRA methods, which often overlook the impact of both system heterogeneity (varying computational and communication capabilities of clients) and data heterogeneity (non-i.i.d. data distribution across clients) on the overall training efficiency, particularly wall-clock time.

The key contributions include:

1.  **New Convergence Bound:** A theoretical convergence bound for Federated LoRA with arbitrary sketching ratios and independent client sampling probabilities, relaxing stringent bounded gradient assumptions.
2.  **Adaptive Bandwidth Allocation:**  A scheme for adaptive bandwidth allocation, considering heterogeneous client resources and system bandwidth constraints.
3.  **Joint Optimization:** A non-convex optimization problem to jointly determine LoRA sketching ratios and sampling probabilities to minimize wall-clock convergence time.  An efficient algorithm is proposed to approximate the solution.
4.  **Experimental Validation:**  Extensive experiments demonstrating significant reductions in wall-clock training time compared to state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates several novel contributions. Deriving a convergence bound without the bounded gradient assumption is a significant theoretical advancement. The adaptive bandwidth allocation and the joint optimization of sketching ratios and sampling probabilities are practical and useful innovations in the context of Federated LoRA. While other papers have explored heterogeneous LoRA, this one explicitly addresses *wall-clock* convergence and tackles it with a theoretically grounded optimization framework. The independent client sampling is also a useful feature for realistic FL systems. The authors are not the first to work on FL + LoRA, nor are they the first to work on either data or system heterogeneity in FL. The novelty comes in the *combination* of these features, along with the theoretical analysis.

*   **Significance:** Efficiently fine-tuning LLMs in FL is a crucial problem for enabling privacy-preserving on-device AI. By addressing both data and system heterogeneity, the paper offers a potentially significant improvement in the practicality and scalability of Federated LoRA.  The reduced wall-clock time is particularly important for real-world applications where faster training is essential. The convergence analysis provides valuable insights for understanding the behavior of Federated LoRA in heterogeneous environments. The experiments, while limited to one dataset, are convincing.

*   **Strengths:** The paper's strengths lie in its sound theoretical analysis, practical problem formulation, and well-designed experiments. The adaptive algorithm is designed with low computational complexity, making it suitable for resource-constrained edge devices. The paper is well-written and organized.

*   **Weaknesses:** The primary weakness is that the experimental evaluation focuses on a single dataset (Commonsense170K).  The experiments would be strengthened by including a broader range of datasets and models to demonstrate the generalizability of the approach. While theoretical analysis is strong, empirical verification across diverse settings is paramount.  The assumption that computation time is constant and measurable for each client might be a simplification in some realistic scenarios, and the paper could discuss the limitations of this assumption. The assumption that the selected clients are allocated with orthogonal bandwidths can also be unrealistic in a congested wireless network. It would be good to see how the adaptive algorithm would work with different bandwidth allocation strategies.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of Federated Learning, particularly in the context of LLMs.  The adaptive strategy and theoretical analysis provide a solid foundation for future research in this area.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:** The paper makes a meaningful contribution to the field by addressing critical limitations of Federated LoRA and proposing a theoretically grounded and practically useful solution. The joint optimization framework and the convergence analysis are notable strengths. However, the limited experimental evaluation on a single dataset and simplifying assumptions about bandwidth allocation and computation time prevent it from receiving a higher score.  While not a groundbreaking theoretical revelation, the *combination* of the features and the strong experimental results warrant a relatively high score. The paper is definitely a solid contribution to the FL community.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Merge Hijacking: Backdoor Attacks to Model Merging of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23561v1)
- **Authors**: Zenghui Yuan, Yangming Xu, Jiawen Shi, Pan Zhou, Lichao Sun
- **Abstract**: Model merging for Large Language Models (LLMs) directly fuses the parameters of different models finetuned on various tasks, creating a unified model for multi-domain tasks. However, due to potential vulnerabilities in models available on open-source platforms, model merging is susceptible to backdoor attacks. In this paper, we propose Merge Hijacking, the first backdoor attack targeting model merging in LLMs. The attacker constructs a malicious upload model and releases it. Once a victim user merges it with any other models, the resulting merged model inherits the backdoor while maintaining utility across tasks. Merge Hijacking defines two main objectives-effectiveness and utility-and achieves them through four steps. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of our attack across different models, merging algorithms, and tasks. Additionally, we show that the attack remains effective even when merging real-world models. Moreover, our attack demonstrates robustness against two inference-time defenses (Paraphrasing and CLEANGEN) and one training-time defense (Fine-pruning).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Merge Hijacking," a novel backdoor attack targeting model merging in large language models (LLMs). The attack involves creating a malicious upload model with a hidden backdoor. When a victim merges this model with other models, the resulting merged model inherits the backdoor, allowing the attacker to trigger specific responses using a predefined trigger while maintaining utility across various tasks. The attack is designed to be effective (achieving high attack success rates) and to maintain utility (preventing detection by users during verification). The paper proposes a four-step approach to achieve these goals, including deriving a backdoor vector, sparsifying it based on magnitude, rescaling, and adding it back to the base model, and finally fine-tuning on a surrogate task. The authors conduct experiments on different LLMs, merging algorithms, and tasks, demonstrating the attack's effectiveness and robustness against several defense mechanisms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a previously unexplored threat vector, namely, backdoor attacks targeting model merging in LLMs. This is a valuable contribution, especially given the increasing popularity of model merging techniques. Defining two core goals, effectiveness and utility, helps make this an intuitive and well-framed project. The methodology to construct the backdoor itself has elements of novelty, even though it builds on existing attack paradigms. The four-step hijacking process seems intuitive and straightforward.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a significant security vulnerability in the growing field of model merging. The potential for attackers to compromise merged models by injecting malicious components has important implications for the deployment and trustworthiness of LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and goals (effectiveness and utility).
    *   Clear and well-structured methodology (four-step Merge Hijacking).
    *   Extensive experimental evaluation across different LLMs, merging algorithms, and tasks.
    *   Robustness testing against inference-time and training-time defenses.
    *   The paper is clearly written and easy to follow.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The backdoor trigger (a rare word "MG") is relatively simple and may be easily detected or filtered out in some applications. Optimizing triggers is still an open issue.
    *   The experimental evaluation, while extensive, could explore more sophisticated defense mechanisms beyond the ones considered in the paper.
    *   The utility goal, while addressed, might benefit from a more rigorous formalization and evaluation, potentially incorporating user studies to assess the perceived utility of the merged models. While ASR remains at 100%, BP degrades with respect to CP which highlights the performance utility degradation. It would be interesting to see how users rate the utility qualitatively.
    *   The assumption that the attacker can only control one malicious upload model might be too simplistic. In real-world scenarios, multiple attackers could collude to inject multiple backdoors, making the attack even more difficult to detect and defend against.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can serve as a starting point for future research on the security of model merging. It can motivate the development of more robust defense mechanisms and contribute to a better understanding of the risks associated with merging models from untrusted sources.

**Justification of Score:**

Overall, this is a solid paper introducing a relevant and timely threat. The methodology is sound, and the experimental results convincingly demonstrate the attack's effectiveness and the limitations of existing defenses. While there are weaknesses, particularly in trigger sophistication and utility evaluations, the novelty and significance of the problem addressed justify a good rating.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Segment Policy Optimization: Effective Segment-Level Credit Assignment in RL for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23564v1)
- **Authors**: Yiran Guo, Lijie Xu, Jie Liu, Dan Ye, Shuang Qiu
- **Abstract**: Enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models effectively using reinforcement learning (RL) remains a crucial challenge. Existing approaches primarily adopt two contrasting advantage estimation granularities: Token-level methods (e.g., PPO) aim to provide the fine-grained advantage signals but suffer from inaccurate estimation due to difficulties in training an accurate critic model. On the other extreme, trajectory-level methods (e.g., GRPO) solely rely on a coarse-grained advantage signal from the final reward, leading to imprecise credit assignment. To address these limitations, we propose Segment Policy Optimization (SPO), a novel RL framework that leverages segment-level advantage estimation at an intermediate granularity, achieving a better balance by offering more precise credit assignment than trajectory-level methods and requiring fewer estimation points than token-level methods, enabling accurate advantage estimation based on Monte Carlo (MC) without a critic model. SPO features three components with novel strategies: (1) flexible segment partition; (2) accurate segment advantage estimation; and (3) policy optimization using segment advantages, including a novel probability-mask strategy. We further instantiate SPO for two specific scenarios: (1) SPO-chain for short chain-of-thought (CoT), featuring novel cutpoint-based partition and chain-based advantage estimation, achieving $6$-$12$ percentage point improvements in accuracy over PPO and GRPO on GSM8K. (2) SPO-tree for long CoT, featuring novel tree-based advantage estimation, which significantly reduces the cost of MC estimation, achieving $7$-$11$ percentage point improvements over GRPO on MATH500 under 2K and 4K context evaluation. We make our code publicly available at https://github.com/AIFrameResearch/SPO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Segment Policy Optimization (SPO), a novel reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to improve the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). SPO addresses limitations in existing RL approaches that use either token-level or trajectory-level advantage estimation. SPO operates at a segment level, partitioning generated sequences into contiguous segments and estimating advantages at this intermediate granularity. This approach balances precise credit assignment with efficient computation. The framework consists of flexible segment partitioning, segment advantage estimation via Monte Carlo sampling, and policy optimization using segment advantages.  The paper presents two specialized instances of SPO: SPO-chain for short chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning and SPO-tree for long CoT reasoning.  Experiments on GSM8K and MATH500 demonstrate improved performance compared to Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). A probability-mask policy optimization strategy is also proposed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of segment-level advantage estimation represents a significant and practical contribution. Moving beyond the extremes of token-level (PPO) and trajectory-level (GRPO) methods is a well-motivated and compelling direction. The specific techniques integrated within SPO, such as the cutpoint-based segment partition, tree-based segment advantage estimation, and probability mask, add further to the paper's novelty. While some elements are inspired by related works (e.g., VinePPO, which is viewed as a special case within SPO), the overall design and integration within the SPO framework are novel. The tree-based sampling approach is a notable contribution that significantly reduces the sampling overhead for long contexts.

*   **Significance:** Improving RL for LLMs remains a critical challenge. The paper provides a practical solution that offers tangible benefits. The experimental results demonstrate that SPO achieves state-of-the-art performance on established benchmarks. The efficiency gains from using MC-based segment-level advantage estimation without a dedicated critic model are also significant, potentially making RL training more accessible on limited resources. The modular architecture increases its applicability to different tasks and scenarios. The paper's significance stems from offering a robust and efficient technique to refine the reasoning capabilities of LLMs through RL.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The motivation for segment-level estimation is logical and supported by experimental evidence.
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The SPO framework is well-designed, with clear components and strategies.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments demonstrate tangible improvements on standard benchmarks.
    *   **Practical Efficiency:** The method avoids a critic model, reducing memory footprint and computational cost.
    *   **Tree-based MC sampling**: Tree-based MC sampling enhances sampling efficiency and contributes substantially.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** While the paper clearly explains the different SPO components, it also includes a degree of complexity.
    *   **Reliance on MC Sampling:** MC estimation becomes expensive as the segment size increases because the computational cost is directly related to how many segments are utilized.
    *   **limited experimental settings**: the authors only limited their study to mathematical tasks. Further experiments are needed to determine its effectiveness in broader application scenarios such as code generation and RLHF.

*   **Potential Influence:** The SPO framework has the potential to influence how RL is applied to train LLMs. The segment-level estimation strategy could become a standard technique in the field, particularly for tasks requiring complex reasoning. The specific implementations (SPO-chain and SPO-tree) provide a practical starting point for researchers and practitioners. The paper will likely motivate further research on alternative segment partitioning strategies and the integration of process rewards with segment advantages.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of RL for LLMs. The segment-level advantage estimation approach is well-motivated, effectively addresses limitations in existing methods, and is supported by robust experimental results. The efficiency gains and modularity of the SPO framework make it a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners. Although it may require careful tuning for deployment and contains aspects that can be improved, the paper has a potential to significantly impact LLM training using RL.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Uni-MuMER: Unified Multi-Task Fine-Tuning of Vision-Language Model for Handwritten Mathematical Expression Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23566v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Li, Jin Jiang, Jianhua Zhu, Shuai Peng, Baole Wei, Yuxuan Zhou, Liangcai Gao
- **Abstract**: Handwritten Mathematical Expression Recognition (HMER) remains a persistent challenge in Optical Character Recognition (OCR) due to the inherent freedom of symbol layout and variability in handwriting styles. Prior methods have faced performance bottlenecks, proposing isolated architectural modifications that are difficult to integrate coherently into a unified framework. Meanwhile, recent advances in pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) have demonstrated strong cross-task generalization, offering a promising foundation for developing unified solutions. In this paper, we introduce Uni-MuMER, which fully fine-tunes a VLM for the HMER task without modifying its architecture, effectively injecting domain-specific knowledge into a generalist framework. Our method integrates three data-driven tasks: Tree-Aware Chain-of-Thought (Tree-CoT) for structured spatial reasoning, Error-Driven Learning (EDL) for reducing confusion among visually similar characters, and Symbol Counting (SC) for improving recognition consistency in long expressions. Experiments on the CROHME and HME100K datasets show that Uni-MuMER achieves new state-of-the-art performance, surpassing the best lightweight specialized model SSAN by 16.31% and the top-performing VLM Gemini2.5-flash by 24.42% in the zero-shot setting. Our datasets, models, and code are open-sourced at: https://github.com/BFlameSwift/Uni-MuMER
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Uni-MuMER, a unified multi-task fine-tuning framework for Handwritten Mathematical Expression Recognition (HMER). It addresses challenges like symbol layout freedom and handwriting variability by leveraging pre-trained vision-language models (VLMs). Uni-MuMER fine-tunes a VLM (Qwen2.5-VL) using three data-driven tasks: Tree-Aware Chain-of-Thought (Tree-CoT) for spatial reasoning, Error-Driven Learning (EDL) to reduce confusion among visually similar characters, and Symbol Counting (SC) to improve recognition consistency.  Experiments on CROHME and HME100K datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, significantly surpassing existing specialized models and general-purpose VLMs. The method also boasts superior inference speed compared to traditional approaches. The authors open-source their code, models, and datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of fully fine-tuning a large VLM for HMER is novel, especially when compared to earlier approaches that relied on architecture modifications. The integration of Tree-CoT, EDL, and SC as *training tasks* tailored for HMER is a strong contribution, facilitating the injection of domain-specific knowledge into a generalist model. The Tree-CoT for explicit spatial reasoning offers a unique approach to capturing the structural aspects of mathematical notation. However, the individual components (e.g., Chain of Thought, error correction by language models) are themselves not entirely new, it's the application and packaging into a unified HMER framework that provides the novelty. This means, while creative, the paper draws inspiration from other areas, potentially diminishing its groundbreaking originality.

*   **Significance:**  Achieving state-of-the-art results on established benchmarks like CROHME is a clear indication of the paper's significance.  The performance gains over existing methods, especially lightweight models, are substantial (e.g., 16.31% over SSAN).  The ability to outperform even the Gemini2.5-flash model in zero-shot learning, after fine-tuning, highlights the effectiveness of the proposed method. The improvement is noticeable, indicating a strong impact on the HMER field. More importantly, it offers a paradigm shift from bespoke architectures to fine-tuning powerful generalist models. The practical benefit of faster inference times further enhances its potential impact. The rigorous ablation studies provide strong evidence for the importance of each component. The thorough analysis of how data diversity impacts performance is also valuable.

*   **Limitations:**
    *   While the paper introduces three novel data-driven tasks, it is important to acknowledge that these tasks are developed with significant manual efforts. This may reduce the transferability of the framework to other OCR problems where similar high-quality labels are difficult to acquire.
    *   The reliance on Qwen2.5-VL as the base model raises questions regarding the generalizability to other VLM architectures. Furthermore, the results are presented in a rapidly evolving VLM landscape; more recent model updates or alternative models might yield different outcomes.
    *   The evaluation, while extensive, is mainly limited to ExpRate and CDM.  While CDM is a step in the right direction, these metrics may not fully capture all aspects of visual equivalence and correctness in complex mathematical expressions.
    *   It would be better if the paper compares the model with large-scale training datasets, and discuss the training complexity.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper makes a substantial contribution to HMER by introducing a unified multi-task fine-tuning framework that effectively leverages large VLMs. While it builds upon existing concepts, the specific application and the novel combination of training tasks lead to significant performance improvements and offer a new direction for the field. The careful evaluation and thorough analysis further strengthen the paper's impact.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper demonstrates significant performance gains on HMER benchmarks, offers a novel approach (fine-tuning VLMs with specialized tasks), and provides a strong ablation analysis. The method's superior inference speed and the open-sourcing of resources further enhance its impact. However, some limitations exist: the reliance on a specific VLM architecture, and the dependence on curated datasets which could present generalizability issues. While the components themselves aren't revolutionary, their integration and application *to HMER* justifies a high score. It isn't quite groundbreaking enough for a 9 or 10.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Evaluating AI capabilities in detecting conspiracy theories on YouTube
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23570v1)
- **Authors**: Leonardo La Rocca, Francesco Corso, Francesco Pierri
- **Abstract**: As a leading online platform with a vast global audience, YouTube's extensive reach also makes it susceptible to hosting harmful content, including disinformation and conspiracy theories. This study explores the use of open-weight Large Language Models (LLMs), both text-only and multimodal, for identifying conspiracy theory videos shared on YouTube. Leveraging a labeled dataset of thousands of videos, we evaluate a variety of LLMs in a zero-shot setting and compare their performance to a fine-tuned RoBERTa baseline. Results show that text-based LLMs achieve high recall but lower precision, leading to increased false positives. Multimodal models lag behind their text-only counterparts, indicating limited benefits from visual data integration. To assess real-world applicability, we evaluate the most accurate models on an unlabeled dataset, finding that RoBERTa achieves performance close to LLMs with a larger number of parameters. Our work highlights the strengths and limitations of current LLM-based approaches for online harmful content detection, emphasizing the need for more precise and robust systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of Large Language Models (LLMs), both text-based and multimodal, in detecting conspiracy theories on YouTube. The authors leverage the YouNICon dataset, enrich it with additional metadata (transcripts, comments, thumbnails), and evaluate the zero-shot performance of several open-weight LLMs. They compare these LLMs against a fine-tuned RoBERTa baseline. The results indicate that while text-based LLMs can achieve high recall, they often suffer from low precision, leading to many false positives. Multimodal models don't perform as well as text-only models, suggesting that visual data integration isn't particularly beneficial for this task. The paper also examines the impact of prompting strategies and ensemble methods. Finally, the best-performing models are applied to an unlabeled dataset of YouTube videos to assess their real-world applicability. They find RoBERTa performing on-par with much larger LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevant Problem:** The paper addresses a significant and timely issue – the spread of conspiracy theories on social media platforms like YouTube. The automated detection of such content is crucial for mitigating potential harm.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study provides a thorough evaluation of various LLMs, including both text-based and multimodal models, comparing their performance in a zero-shot setting. The benchmarking against a fine-tuned RoBERTa baseline provides a valuable point of comparison.
    *   **Real-World Assessment:** The application of the models to an unlabeled dataset and subsequent manual annotation offers insights into the real-world applicability of these approaches.
    *   **Cost-Sensitive Evaluation:** The use of the Normalized Expected Cost (NEC) metric allows for a nuanced analysis of model performance under different cost assumptions (e.g., different penalties for false positives and false negatives).
    *   **Detailed Analysis:**  The paper includes error analysis, prompting strategy analysis, and ensemble modeling, presenting a multifaceted view of the challenges and opportunities.
    *   **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making it easy to follow the methodology and results.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** The application of LLMs to misinformation detection is not entirely novel.  While the specific focus on YouTube conspiracy theories and the evaluation of several recent open-weight models is valuable, the core idea has been explored in previous research.  The methods used for prompting and evaluation, though sound, are largely standard.
    *   **Multimodal Limitations:** The multimodal component is somewhat limited. The use of only thumbnail images as visual input may not be sufficient to capture the full potential of multimodal approaches. Analyzing video frames or employing more advanced visual analysis techniques could have yielded more significant insights.
    *   **Dataset Bias:** The YouNICon dataset, while valuable, may introduce biases related to the specific channels included and the annotation process. Also using a dataset of videos licensed under Creative Commons is not fully representative of YouTube content. This limits the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Zero-Shot Limitations:** While zero-shot learning is useful, the models' performance might improve significantly with fine-tuning on a larger, more diverse dataset of conspiracy-related content.
    *   **Limited Error Analysis:** While error analysis is provided, it is relatively shallow. A more in-depth exploration of specific types of conspiracy theories that are easily detected or missed would be beneficial.
    *   **Reproducibility:** While the authors have made their enrichment pipeline available, providing all the exact prompts and other settings may allow to fully reproduce their results.
*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper offers practical insights into the capabilities and limitations of current open-weight LLMs for detecting conspiracy theories on YouTube.
    *   The findings can inform the development of more effective content moderation strategies.
    *   The use of cost-sensitive evaluation metrics provides a valuable framework for decision-making in real-world applications.
    *   The identification of the trade-offs between precision and recall highlights the importance of carefully considering the specific goals and context of content moderation efforts.
    *   The error analysis sheds light on the challenges of classifying conspiracy content and suggests avenues for future research.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is well-executed and addresses a relevant problem. However, its novelty is limited by prior work in the field. The core methodology (zero-shot LLM classification) is not particularly innovative, although the evaluation is thorough. The multimodal component could have been more robust. A more detailed error analysis would have been beneficial. The dataset limitations also impact the generalizability of the findings.

Considering these strengths and weaknesses, a score of 6 is appropriate. The paper makes a solid contribution to the field, but its novelty is not substantial enough to warrant a higher score. It provides valuable insights into the performance of current LLMs and points the way for future research, but it does not represent a groundbreaking advancement.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### CoT Red-Handed: Stress Testing Chain-of-Thought Monitoring
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23575v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Arnav, Pablo Bernabeu-Pérez, Nathan Helm-Burger, Tim Kostolansky, Hannes Whittingham, Mary Phuong
- **Abstract**: As AI models are deployed with increasing autonomy, it is important to ensure they do not take harmful actions unnoticed. As a potential mitigation, we investigate Chain-of-Thought (CoT) monitoring, wherein a weaker trusted monitor model continuously oversees the intermediate reasoning steps of a more powerful but untrusted model. We compare CoT monitoring to action-only monitoring, where only final outputs are reviewed, in a red-teaming setup where the untrusted model is instructed to pursue harmful side tasks while completing a coding problem. We find that CoT monitoring improves detection by up to 27 percentage points in scenarios where action-only monitoring fails to reliably identify sabotage. However, CoT traces can also contain misleading rationalizations that deceive the monitor, reducing performance in more obvious sabotage cases. To address this, we introduce a hybrid protocol that independently scores both reasoning and final outputs and combines them using a weighted average. This hybrid monitor consistently outperforms both CoT and action-only monitors across all tested models and tasks, with detection rates over four times higher than action-only monitoring for subtle deception scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) monitoring as a method to detect harmful actions taken by AI models, specifically in a red-teaming setup where models are instructed to complete coding tasks while pursuing hidden, subversive side tasks.  The authors compare CoT monitoring, where a weaker model oversees the reasoning process, to action-only monitoring, which only reviews the final outputs.  They find that CoT monitoring improves detection rates in subtle sabotage scenarios but can be misled by deceptive reasoning.  To address this, they introduce a hybrid approach combining both reasoning and final output analysis, which consistently outperforms both individual methods across various models and tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to AI safety by systematically evaluating CoT monitoring in an adversarial setting with specifically designed attack policies.  While CoT monitoring itself is not a new concept, the application of it within a formal AI control framework *and* the introduction of the hybrid monitoring approach represents a significant step forward.  Previous work had not thoroughly explored the trade-offs between CoT monitoring and action-only monitoring, nor had they assessed the ability of untrusted models to deceive CoT monitors in the manner explored by this paper. Moreover, the proxy task of subtle sabotage in code is a useful simplification that allows for controlled red-teaming. The exploration of attack policies designed to evade detection strengthens the evaluation.

**Significance:**  The findings have important implications for the development of safer and more robust AI systems.  The paper's results provide valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different monitoring approaches, which can inform the design of more effective AI control mechanisms.  The demonstration of CoT monitoring's vulnerability to deceptive reasoning is a crucial contribution, highlighting the need for more sophisticated monitoring techniques. The hybrid monitor is also an important contribution to improve safety.  The controlled experimental setting offers a solid foundation for future research. Moreover, by focusing on monitoring effectiveness in the presence of capability gaps (weaker monitors overseeing more powerful untrusted models), the work increases its potential applicability to future, more advanced AI systems. The study's controlled nature could also allow other researchers to reproduce these findings.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Task Domain:**  The study is limited to coding tasks.  While coding provides a structured environment for analysis, it may not fully generalize to other domains with more complex and nuanced behaviors. Other safety-critical domains such as autonomous driving or healthcare diagnostics could be more interesting to explore.

*   **Proxy Tasks:** The side tasks, while designed to represent potential misalignments, are still proxy tasks. Actual malicious goals might be more complex and harder to detect than what is explored here.

*   **Monitor Capability:** While using weaker monitors is practical, the specific capabilities and limitations of those monitors heavily influence outcomes. The selection of GPT-4.1-mini and other models for the role of trusted monitor should be more clearly justified. Also, the monitors have 1% false positive rate which could be costly in practice and is sensitive to the selection process.

*   **Evaluation Awareness:** The paper mentions recent work raising concerns about models being aware they are being evaluated. Addressing this limitation might provide a more accurate reflection of monitoring in real-world scenarios.

**Justification of the Score:**

The paper is a strong contribution. It directly addresses a crucial problem in AI safety - how to reliably detect misaligned behavior in advanced models. The hybrid monitor approach is a promising step towards developing more robust AI control mechanisms. The experiments are well-designed, and the analysis is thorough. However, the paper's limited task domain and use of proxy tasks, as well as limited attention to monitor capabilities and evaluation awareness, prevent it from achieving a higher score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Cognitive Guardrails for Open-World Decision Making in Autonomous Drone Swarms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23576v1)
- **Authors**: Jane Cleland-Huang, Pedro Antonio Alarcon Granadeno, Arturo Miguel Russell Bernal, Demetrius Hernandez, Michael Murphy, Maureen Petterson, Walter Scheirer
- **Abstract**: Small Uncrewed Aerial Systems (sUAS) are increasingly deployed as autonomous swarms in search-and-rescue and other disaster-response scenarios. In these settings, they use computer vision (CV) to detect objects of interest and autonomously adapt their missions. However, traditional CV systems often struggle to recognize unfamiliar objects in open-world environments or to infer their relevance for mission planning. To address this, we incorporate large language models (LLMs) to reason about detected objects and their implications. While LLMs can offer valuable insights, they are also prone to hallucinations and may produce incorrect, misleading, or unsafe recommendations. To ensure safe and sensible decision-making under uncertainty, high-level decisions must be governed by cognitive guardrails. This article presents the design, simulation, and real-world integration of these guardrails for sUAS swarms in search-and-rescue missions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CAIRN, a framework designed to improve decision-making in autonomous drone swarms operating in open-world environments, specifically in search and rescue (SAR) missions.  CAIRN integrates Bayesian belief updates, large language models (LLMs) for contextual reasoning, and cognitive guardrails to ensure safe and sensible actions. The system uses LLMs to interpret clues discovered through computer vision, combining this information with a Bayesian model that tracks the probabilities of different search strategies.  Cognitive guardrails, including belief entropy, cost-benefit analysis, ethical/regulatory oversight, and a safety envelope, constrain the swarm's behavior, preventing unsafe or illogical actions. The paper describes the design, simulation, and planned integration of CAIRN into a real-world drone platform.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a significant effort to tackle a complex and relevant problem: ensuring safety and reliability of autonomous drone swarms in unpredictable environments. The integration of Bayesian inference with LLMs and a layered safety framework is relatively novel. While each individual component (Bayesian models, LLMs, safety constraints) is not entirely new, the *combination* and application to autonomous drone swarms for SAR missions represent a significant advancement. Specifically, the way they use the LLM alongside computer vision and then apply several safety guardrails seems novel.
*   **Significance:** The problem addressed is of significant importance. The deployment of autonomous drone swarms in SAR and other disaster response scenarios has the potential to save lives and improve the efficiency of rescue operations. However, the reliance on imperfect sensors and the inherent uncertainty of open-world environments necessitate robust decision-making frameworks and effective safety mechanisms.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem and provides a well-structured solution in CAIRN.
    *   The integration of LLMs for scene understanding is a valuable addition to traditional CV approaches, especially in open-world scenarios.
    *   The layered safety framework (cognitive guardrails and safety envelope) demonstrates a strong focus on safety and accountability.
    *   The use of a Bayesian model for tracking search strategy probabilities provides a principled approach to dynamic adaptation.
    *   The simulation environment provides a valuable tool for development and validation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies heavily on simulations. A more rigorous validation using real-world data and physical drone deployments would greatly strengthen the findings. The reliance on GPT-4o, while cutting-edge, also introduces uncertainty due to the model's inherent limitations and potential biases.
    *   The specific parameters used in the Bayesian model and the weighting factors used in Eq. 3 appear to be based primarily on the team's best judgment. Quantitative analysis and empirical tuning of these parameters are needed.
    *   While the cognitive guardrails are conceptually sound, the paper lacks a detailed evaluation of their effectiveness in preventing unsafe actions or mitigating potential risks.
    *   The paper describes an "ethical oversight" guardrail, which is great, but it is unclear how these are concretely enforced besides 'expert advisors'. Further detail on how these constraints will inform concrete actions is needed.

*   **Potential Impact:** The CAIRN framework has the potential to significantly advance the field of autonomous robotics and disaster response. If successfully validated in real-world deployments, it could lead to more reliable and effective drone-based SAR operations. The approach could also be adapted to other domains, such as infrastructure inspection and environmental monitoring.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-reasoned and structured framework that addresses a significant problem in autonomous robotics. The novelty lies in the integration of Bayesian inference, LLMs, and a multi-layered safety framework. While the simulation results are promising, the lack of real-world validation and the reliance on subjective parameter settings limit the overall impact. The paper makes a novel contribution, but significant work remains to fully validate and refine the approach.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### On-Policy RL with Optimal Reward Baseline
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23585v1)
- **Authors**: Yaru Hao, Li Dong, Xun Wu, Shaohan Huang, Zewen Chi, Furu Wei
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning algorithms are fundamental to align large language models with human preferences and to enhance their reasoning capabilities. However, current reinforcement learning algorithms often suffer from training instability due to loose on-policy constraints and computational inefficiency due to auxiliary models. In this work, we propose On-Policy RL with Optimal reward baseline (OPO), a novel and simplified reinforcement learning algorithm designed to address these challenges. OPO emphasizes the importance of exact on-policy training, which empirically stabilizes the training process and enhances exploration. Moreover, OPO introduces the optimal reward baseline that theoretically minimizes gradient variance. We evaluate OPO on mathematical reasoning benchmarks. The results demonstrate its superior performance and training stability without additional models or regularization terms. Furthermore, OPO achieves lower policy shifts and higher output entropy, encouraging more diverse and less repetitive responses. These results highlight OPO as a promising direction for stable and effective reinforcement learning in large language model alignment and reasoning tasks. The implementation is provided at https://github.com/microsoft/LMOps/tree/main/opo.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "On-Policy RL with Optimal Reward Baseline" (OPO), a new reinforcement learning algorithm designed to improve the stability and efficiency of aligning large language models (LLMs). OPO emphasizes exact on-policy training to enhance exploration and stabilize the training process. It also derives an optimal reward baseline to minimize gradient variance theoretically. The authors evaluate OPO on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating superior performance and training stability compared to existing methods like PPO and GRPO, without requiring auxiliary models or regularization terms.  Furthermore, OPO leads to lower policy shifts and higher output entropy, resulting in more diverse and less repetitive responses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the combination of exact on-policy training with a theoretically derived optimal reward baseline specifically designed to minimize gradient variance. While on-policy methods and baseline techniques are not entirely new, the rigorous derivation of an *optimal* baseline within an exact on-policy framework presents a unique approach. The simplicity of the resulting algorithm, avoiding auxiliary models and regularization, is also a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing the key challenges of instability and inefficiency in current RLHF algorithms. The results show promising improvements in performance and training stability. The lower policy shifts and higher entropy outputs are particularly important as they tackle the alignment tax problem and promote more diverse and creative language generation. Given the widespread use of RLHF in aligning LLMs, a more stable and efficient algorithm like OPO could have a substantial impact.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The theoretical justification for the optimal reward baseline is strong.
    *   The experimental results clearly demonstrate improved performance and stability.
    *   The analysis of policy shifts and entropy provides valuable insights.
    *   The algorithm is relatively simple and easy to implement.
    *   The code is made available.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experiments are primarily focused on mathematical reasoning tasks. While these tasks are relevant, expanding the evaluation to other domains (e.g., dialogue generation, summarization) would increase the generality of the findings.
    *   The rule-based reward function may limit the applicability of the algorithm. Testing with more complex reward models, especially human preference models, would further validate its real-world performance.
    *   While exact on-policy training offers advantages, it may be computationally expensive. More detailed analysis of the computational cost compared to off-policy methods is needed.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has a strong potential to influence the field.  OPO offers a promising avenue for building more stable and efficient RLHF pipelines. The emphasis on exact on-policy training could inspire further research into this less explored area.  The simplicity of OPO also makes it accessible to a wider audience, potentially accelerating its adoption.

*   **Further research:** OPO only focus on the policy optimization, the interaction of OPO with reward training methods needs more exploration.

*   **Rigor:** The paper has high rigor, especially in its analysis of variance reduction and on-policy training.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-justified algorithm with promising results. The theoretical derivation of the optimal reward baseline and the empirical demonstration of improved stability and diversity are significant contributions. The code availability further strengthens the impact. However, the somewhat limited scope of the experimental evaluation and the reliance on a rule-based reward function hold it back from being a truly exceptional contribution (scoring above 9). More thorough ablation and comparison studies on multiple tasks would improve this work. Nevertheless, the paper is a strong and valuable addition to the literature on reinforcement learning and language model alignment.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Jigsaw-R1: A Study of Rule-based Visual Reinforcement Learning with Jigsaw Puzzles
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23590v1)
