- **Authors**: Zifu Wang, Junyi Zhu, Bo Tang, Zhiyu Li, Feiyu Xiong, Jiaqian Yu, Matthew B. Blaschko
- **Abstract**: The application of rule-based reinforcement learning (RL) to multimodal large language models (MLLMs) introduces unique challenges and potential deviations from findings in text-only domains, particularly for perception-heavy tasks. This paper provides a comprehensive study of rule-based visual RL using jigsaw puzzles as a structured experimental framework, revealing several key findings. \textit{Firstly,} we find that MLLMs, initially performing near to random guessing on simple puzzles, achieve near-perfect accuracy and generalize to complex, unseen configurations through fine-tuning. \textit{Secondly,} training on jigsaw puzzles can induce generalization to other visual tasks, with effectiveness tied to specific task configurations. \textit{Thirdly,} MLLMs can learn and generalize with or without explicit reasoning, though open-source models often favor direct answering. Consequently, even when trained for step-by-step reasoning, they can ignore the thinking process in deriving the final answer. \textit{Fourthly,} we observe that complex reasoning patterns appear to be pre-existing rather than emergent, with their frequency increasing alongside training and task difficulty. \textit{Finally,} our results demonstrate that RL exhibits more effective generalization than Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), and an initial SFT cold start phase can hinder subsequent RL optimization. Although these observations are based on jigsaw puzzles and may vary across other visual tasks, this research contributes a valuable piece of jigsaw to the larger puzzle of collective understanding rule-based visual RL and its potential in multimodal learning. The code is available at: \href{https://github.com/zifuwanggg/Jigsaw-R1}{https://github.com/zifuwanggg/Jigsaw-R1}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Jigsaw-R1: A Study of Rule-based Visual Reinforcement Learning with Jigsaw Puzzles":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates rule-based Reinforcement Learning (RL) for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) using jigsaw puzzles as a structured visual task. The authors study how MLLMs perform on jigsaw puzzles, whether training on puzzles leads to generalizable abilities for other visual tasks, the role of explicit reasoning, the emergence of complex reasoning patterns, and how RL compares to Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) in terms of generalization. The key findings are: 1) MLLMs, initially poor at simple puzzles, achieve near-perfect accuracy after fine-tuning and generalize to more complex, unseen puzzle configurations. 2) Training on puzzles can induce generalization to other visual tasks, depending on task configurations. 3) MLLMs can learn with or without explicit reasoning, but open-source models often favor direct answering, even when trained for step-by-step reasoning. 4) Complex reasoning patterns are pre-existing rather than emergent, increasing with training and task difficulty. 5) RL exhibits better generalization than SFT, and SFT cold start phases can hinder subsequent RL optimization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic examination of rule-based visual RL within a structured framework of jigsaw puzzles for MLLMs. While jigsaw puzzles and rule-based RL are not entirely new concepts, the application of this combination to analyze the reasoning processes and generalization capabilities of *modern* MLLMs in the *visual* domain is a valuable contribution.  The study's focus on analyzing the 'aha' moment and comparing RL with SFT in this context adds further distinction.  The exploration of prompting strategies to elicit or suppress explicit reasoning is also a novel aspect.
*   **Significance:** The paper makes several important contributions to the field of multimodal learning. The finding that MLLMs can generalize from jigsaw puzzle training to other visual tasks has significant practical implications. The analysis of reasoning strategies (explicit vs. direct answering) provides valuable insights into the internal workings of MLLMs and suggests ways to optimize their performance. The comparison of RL and SFT is also crucial for guiding future research on training MLLMs. The observations about pre-existing reasoning patterns challenge the notion of emergent abilities, warranting further investigation. The limitations section is well written, and helps point towards ways future work can validate, invalidate, or extend these findings.

**Strengths:**

*   **Systematic Approach:** The paper employs a structured experimental framework that allows for careful control and analysis of different factors affecting MLLM performance.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The authors address a wide range of research questions related to rule-based visual RL, covering both performance and reasoning aspects.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, with clear explanations of the methodology, results, and conclusions.
*   **Strong Empirical Evidence:** The authors provide extensive experimental results to support their claims.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The findings have practical implications for training and deploying MLLMs in visual tasks.
*   **Reproducibility:** The code is publicly available.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Task Specificity:** The results are based on jigsaw puzzles, which are a specific type of visual task. While the authors argue for their task selection, the generalizability of some findings to other visual tasks is not guaranteed and may depend on the specific task characteristics.
*   **Model Limitations:** While various MLLMs are used, there's no guarantee that trends observed here extend to models yet to be produced.
*   **Metric Limitations:** Although the authors quantify the 'aha moment' emergence, there may be aspects of reasoning that are missed by the keyword analysis method. The interpretation of keyword analysis can be subjective.
*   **Lack of Ablation Studies:** While there is comparison of RL and SFT, and differing reward types, an additional exploration of the interplay between each hyperparameter in GRPO (Shao et al. 2024), could have increased understanding.
*   **Scale** The scale of experiments may be limited for some of the less explored analyses. For example, tool-use is mentioned in a very cursory way.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7. The paper provides a valuable and systematic exploration of rule-based visual RL for MLLMs using jigsaw puzzles. The experimental setup is well-designed, and the analysis is comprehensive. The findings provide important insights into the reasoning processes and generalization capabilities of MLLMs in visual tasks. However, the task-specificity of jigsaw puzzles and the model variations does limit the broad applicability of some of the conclusions, and the lack of more complex investigations prevents a higher score. While the results offer intriguing insights, the scope of the study could be further broadened to solidify its impact across the field.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### MAPLE: A Mobile Assistant with Persistent Finite State Machines for Recovery Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23596v1)
- **Authors**: Linqiang Guo, Wei Liu, Yi Wen Heng, Tse-Hsun, Chen, Yang Wang
- **Abstract**: Mobile GUI agents aim to autonomously complete user-instructed tasks across mobile apps. Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) enable these agents to interpret UI screens, identify actionable elements, and perform interactions such as tapping or typing. However, existing agents remain reactive: they reason only over the current screen and lack a structured model of app navigation flow, limiting their ability to understand context, detect unexpected outcomes, and recover from errors. We present MAPLE, a state-aware multi-agent framework that abstracts app interactions as a Finite State Machine (FSM). We computationally model each UI screen as a discrete state and user actions as transitions, allowing the FSM to provide a structured representation of the app execution. MAPLE consists of specialized agents responsible for four phases of task execution: planning, execution, verification, error recovery, and knowledge retention. These agents collaborate to dynamically construct FSMs in real time based on perception data extracted from the UI screen, allowing the GUI agents to track navigation progress and flow, validate action outcomes through pre- and post-conditions of the states, and recover from errors by rolling back to previously stable states. Our evaluation results on two challenging cross-app benchmarks, Mobile-Eval-E and SPA-Bench, show that MAPLE outperforms the state-of-the-art baseline, improving task success rate by up to 12%, recovery success by 13.8%, and action accuracy by 6.5%. Our results highlight the importance of structured state modeling in guiding mobile GUI agents during task execution. Moreover, our FSM representation can be integrated into future GUI agent architectures as a lightweight, model-agnostic memory layer to support structured planning, execution verification, and error recovery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MAPLE: A Mobile Assistant with Persistent Finite State Machines for Recovery Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MAPLE, a mobile GUI agent that leverages Finite State Machines (FSMs) to improve its ability to complete user-instructed tasks across mobile apps. Unlike existing reactive GUI agents that only reason based on the current screen, MAPLE models app interactions as an FSM, where UI screens are states and user actions are transitions. This state-aware representation allows MAPLE to track navigation progress, validate action outcomes using pre- and post-conditions, and recover from errors by reverting to previously stable states. MAPLE consists of specialized agents for planning, execution, verification/recovery, and knowledge retention.  The authors evaluate MAPLE on two challenging cross-app benchmarks (Mobile-Eval-E and SPA-Bench) and demonstrate significant improvements in task success rate, recovery success, and action accuracy compared to a state-of-the-art baseline.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in applying FSM modeling to mobile GUI agents to achieve state-aware reasoning. While FSMs are a well-established concept, their integration into GUI agents for improved error recovery and context understanding is a significant contribution. The paper is the first to explicitly model GUI agent behavior as a FSM.

*   **Significance:** The ability to recover from errors and maintain context is crucial for practical GUI agents. MAPLE's FSM-based approach addresses a key limitation of current reactive agents and contributes to more robust and reliable task execution. The improved performance demonstrated on standard benchmarks validates the effectiveness of the approach. The results show that using FSM improves agents’ ability to track and recover during task execution. Additionally, the framework is designed to be model-agnostic, adding the FSM layer on top of existing agent architectures.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    *   FSM improves on the reactive nature of the current state-of-the-art methods.
    *   Significant performance improvements over a strong baseline on challenging benchmarks.
    *   The agent framework is modular and potentially compatible with existing agent architectures.
    *   Thorough ablation studies demonstrate the importance of each component in MAPLE.
    *   The results when switching the backbone MLLMs show that even with a relatively weaker MLLM the agent performance are significantly improved comparing to the previous state-of-the-art methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper does not directly address scalability concerns for complex apps with numerous UI states, while the current design works well with apps that the transitions are relatively easy and linear, for complex transition it will be very difficult to use a FSM. Also, the FSM construction can be computationally intensive and can be a performance bottleneck
    *   Human evaluation is subjective and can be expensive. More automated evaluation metrics could strengthen the evaluation.
    *   A deeper analysis of failure cases would provide further insights into the limitations of MAPLE.
    *   The paper doesn't compare directly to GUI-Xplore. While it mentions how MAPLE constructs FSMs online during task execution comparing to offline analysis in GUI-Xplore, it is important to analyse if MAPLE learns any structured knowledge that GUI-Xplore can utilize.

*   **Potential Influence:** MAPLE's FSM-based approach has the potential to influence the design of future GUI agents. It provides a valuable framework for improving robustness, context awareness, and error recovery. The modularity of the framework could facilitate the integration of FSM modeling into existing agent architectures.

*   **Overall:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of mobile GUI agents. The FSM-based approach effectively addresses a key limitation of existing agents and leads to substantial performance improvements. The framework is well-designed and thoroughly evaluated. While there are some limitations, the overall strengths of the paper warrant a high score.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** MAPLE introduces a novel FSM based reasoning on the mobile agent which solves the reactive nature of the current state-of-the-art, addressing a significant limitation in the field. The clear performance improvement on complex benchmarks validates the usefulness of the FSM model. This approach sets a new direction of study and is likely to be built on for future mobile agent design. The only concern is how to scale up this method to tasks that have infinite states and transitions, but in general it still significantly improves the current state of the art, and has a clear novelty and significance within the field.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### LLM Performance for Code Generation on Noisy Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23598v1)
- **Authors**: Radzim Sendyka, Christian Cabrera, Andrei Paleyes, Diana Robinson, Neil Lawrence
- **Abstract**: This paper investigates the ability of large language models (LLMs) to recognise and solve tasks which have been obfuscated beyond recognition. Focusing on competitive programming and benchmark tasks (LeetCode and MATH), we compare performance across multiple models and obfuscation methods, such as noise and redaction. We demonstrate that all evaluated LLMs can solve tasks obfuscated to a level where the text would be unintelligible to human readers, and does not contain key pieces of instruction or context. We introduce the concept of eager pattern matching to describe this behaviour, which is not observed in tasks published after the models' knowledge cutoff date, indicating strong memorisation or overfitting to training data, rather than legitimate reasoning about the presented problem. We report empirical evidence of distinct performance decay patterns between contaminated and unseen datasets. We discuss the implications for benchmarking and evaluations of model behaviour, arguing for caution when designing experiments using standard datasets. We also propose measuring the decay of performance under obfuscation as a possible strategy for detecting dataset contamination and highlighting potential safety risks and interpretability issues for automated software systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the performance of large language models (LLMs) on code generation tasks (LeetCode and MATH datasets) that have been deliberately obfuscated. The key finding is that LLMs can often solve tasks obfuscated to a point where they are unintelligible to humans and lack key instructions. The authors introduce the concept of "eager pattern matching," where models solve problems by recognizing superficial similarities to known tasks, even when the task has been altered and the solution is technically incorrect. The study empirically shows that performance decay under extreme obfuscation is an indicator of dataset contamination and overfitting.  The paper contrasts performance on tasks released *before* the models' knowledge cutoff (likely in the training data) versus tasks released *after* the cutoff. It proposes using performance decay under obfuscation as a strategy to detect dataset contamination and to highlight safety risks related to the reliance on memorization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The core idea of using obfuscation to probe for dataset contamination is a significant contribution. While prior works examine LLM resilience to noise, this paper intentionally pushes obfuscation to an extreme level to expose memorization effects rather than focusing on improving robustness.
    *   **Experimental Rigor:** The experimental setup is well-defined and reproducible. The use of multiple datasets, LLMs, and obfuscation methods provides a robust basis for the conclusions. The use of human baselines to establish the limits of human understanding adds valuable context.
    *   **Clear Demonstration of a Problem:** The paper provides compelling evidence of a real problem - that LLMs can appear to solve tasks they don't truly understand.  The adversarial examples solidify the idea that superficial similarity can lead to incorrect solutions.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings have significant implications for the evaluation of LLMs, particularly in safety-critical applications where reliance on memorization can lead to unpredictable and potentially dangerous behavior. The suggested use of obfuscation as a detection strategy has practical value.
    *   **Discussion of Implications:**  The paper does a good job of discussing the implications of these findings for various aspects of LLM deployment, including benchmarking, software engineering practices, and multi-agent systems.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scale of Datasets Used:**  While the experiments are rigorous, the datasets are relatively small (only the first 20 questions from LeetCode and MATH). This raises the question of how well these findings generalize to larger and more diverse datasets.
    *   **Reliance on External Model Access:** The experiments rely on access to external LLM APIs. The lack of control over model architecture and training data can limit the precision of the analysis. It would be more compelling to fine-tune and test the hypothesis on models trained from scratch.
    *   **Simplistic Obfuscation Methods:** The obfuscation methods used (typos, deletions, truncation) are relatively simple. While they effectively demonstrate the effect, more sophisticated obfuscation techniques could reveal more nuanced behavior.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Model-Specific Differences:** Although Appendix C contains some analysis of model-specific differences, the paper could be strengthened by exploring model-specific susceptibility to this phenomenon in more depth. Are certain architectures or training methodologies more prone to eager pattern matching?

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper raises important questions about the validity of current LLM benchmarks.
    *   The proposed obfuscation-based method can be a valuable tool for detecting dataset contamination and assessing the robustness of LLMs.
    *   The research has implications for the safe and reliable deployment of LLMs in real-world applications, especially in software engineering.
    *   It stimulates further research into developing more robust evaluation techniques and understanding the limitations of current LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach to uncovering dataset contamination and overfitting in LLMs.  While some limitations exist, the study's rigor and practical implications are significant. The demonstrated problem of eager pattern matching highlights a critical vulnerability in LLMs, especially in safety-critical domains. The suggested obfuscation technique is valuable for evaluating the robustness of LLMs. Therefore, a score of 8 is warranted, balancing the novelty, impact, and limitations of the work.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### A Comprehensive Evaluation of Multi-Modal Large Language Models for Endoscopy Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23601v1)
- **Authors**: Shengyuan Liu, Boyun Zheng, Wenting Chen, Zhihao Peng, Zhenfei Yin, Jing Shao, Jiancong Hu, Yixuan Yuan
- **Abstract**: Endoscopic procedures are essential for diagnosing and treating internal diseases, and multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) are increasingly applied to assist in endoscopy analysis. However, current benchmarks are limited, as they typically cover specific endoscopic scenarios and a small set of clinical tasks, failing to capture the real-world diversity of endoscopic scenarios and the full range of skills needed in clinical workflows. To address these issues, we introduce EndoBench, the first comprehensive benchmark specifically designed to assess MLLMs across the full spectrum of endoscopic practice with multi-dimensional capacities. EndoBench encompasses 4 distinct endoscopic scenarios, 12 specialized clinical tasks with 12 secondary subtasks, and 5 levels of visual prompting granularities, resulting in 6,832 rigorously validated VQA pairs from 21 diverse datasets. Our multi-dimensional evaluation framework mirrors the clinical workflow--spanning anatomical recognition, lesion analysis, spatial localization, and surgical operations--to holistically gauge the perceptual and diagnostic abilities of MLLMs in realistic scenarios. We benchmark 23 state-of-the-art models, including general-purpose, medical-specialized, and proprietary MLLMs, and establish human clinician performance as a reference standard. Our extensive experiments reveal: (1) proprietary MLLMs outperform open-source and medical-specialized models overall, but still trail human experts; (2) medical-domain supervised fine-tuning substantially boosts task-specific accuracy; and (3) model performance remains sensitive to prompt format and clinical task complexity. EndoBench establishes a new standard for evaluating and advancing MLLMs in endoscopy, highlighting both progress and persistent gaps between current models and expert clinical reasoning. We publicly release our benchmark and code.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "EndoBench: A Comprehensive Evaluation of Multi-Modal Large Language Models for Endoscopy Analysis" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EndoBench, a new, comprehensive benchmark for evaluating multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) in the context of endoscopy analysis. It addresses limitations in existing benchmarks that often focus on specific endoscopic scenarios and a limited set of clinical tasks. EndoBench spans four distinct endoscopic scenarios (Gastroscopy, Colonoscopy, Capsule endoscopy, and Surgical endoscopy), 12 specialized clinical tasks (anatomical recognition, lesion analysis, spatial localization, surgical operations), and five levels of visual prompting granularity, resulting in a dataset of 6,832 rigorously validated VQA pairs from 21 diverse datasets. The authors benchmark 23 state-of-the-art models, including general-purpose, medical-specialized, and proprietary MLLMs. The study reveals that proprietary MLLMs perform best overall, but still lag human experts, and medical domain fine-tuning improves task-specific accuracy. The paper highlights the persistent gap between current models and expert clinical reasoning in endoscopy.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty is the creation of a comprehensive, multi-dimensional benchmark specifically tailored to endoscopy. Prior benchmarks were often narrower in scope, focusing on single tasks or limited endoscopic settings.  EndoBench stands out by its breadth of scenarios, tasks, and visual prompting methods. However, several existing papers focus on VQA for endoscopy (which the paper references), so the *task itself* is not new, but the scale and comprehensiveness *is* a significant contribution. Further, the creation of the new EndoVQA-Instruct dataset is a valuable contribution to the research community.

*   **Significance:** Endoscopy is a critical diagnostic tool, and the increasing use of MLLMs in this area makes robust evaluation vital. EndoBench provides a standardized framework for assessing and comparing different MLLMs, which can accelerate research and development of more effective AI-assisted tools for endoscopy. The benchmark enables a more granular understanding of the strengths and weaknesses of MLLMs, identifying areas where models excel and where they still fall short. The inclusion of a human expert baseline is also critical. However, a limitation is that the work is primarily a benchmark creation/evaluation paper. While it highlights the gap between models and human experts, it doesn't propose a novel *method* for bridging this gap; the *benchmark itself* is intended to facilitate such method development by others.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive and multi-faceted benchmark design.
    *   Spans a diverse set of endoscopic scenarios and clinical tasks.
    *   Includes a large and diverse dataset.
    *   Evaluates a wide range of MLLMs.
    *   Establishes a human expert baseline.
    *   Provides detailed analysis of model performance across different scenarios, tasks, and visual prompting methods.
    *   Publicly releases the benchmark and code, enabling further research and development.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Primarily a benchmark creation and evaluation paper; doesn't propose a novel method for improving MLLMs.
    *   Relies on exact match accuracy as the primary evaluation metric, which might not fully capture the nuances of clinical reasoning.  Alternative metrics (e.g., semantic similarity) could be considered.
    *   Does not focus on 3D video endoscopic analysis.
    *   The analysis could benefit from more in-depth qualitative error analysis to better understand the types of errors that MLLMs are making.
    *   The paper does not directly address any ethical considerations associated with deployment of AI.

*   **Potential Influence:** EndoBench is likely to become a widely used benchmark in the field of medical imaging and MLLMs. It will facilitate more rigorous and standardized evaluation of MLLMs for endoscopy analysis, driving further progress in this area. The findings from the benchmark study will provide valuable insights for researchers and developers, guiding the design and training of more effective MLLMs.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**  EndoBench is a substantial contribution to the field due to its comprehensive nature and standardized framework. The scale and granularity of the benchmark, combined with the human expert baseline, are significant strengths. However, the primary limitation is that it is an evaluation-focused paper rather than a novel method. While the benchmark is extremely valuable for assessing MLLM performance in a crucial medical domain and will likely accelerate future research, the lack of a novel solution or algorithm keeps it from being a truly exceptional contribution deserving of a higher score. Additionally, the paper does not directly address any ethical considerations associated with deployment of AI.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Muddit: Liberating Generation Beyond Text-to-Image with a Unified Discrete Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23606v1)
- **Authors**: Qingyu Shi, Jinbin Bai, Zhuoran Zhao, Wenhao Chai, Kaidong Yu, Jianzong Wu, Shuangyong Song, Yunhai Tong, Xiangtai Li, Xuelong Li, Shuicheng Yan
- **Abstract**: Unified generation models aim to handle diverse tasks across modalities -- such as text generation, image generation, and vision-language reasoning -- within a single architecture and decoding paradigm. Autoregressive unified models suffer from slow inference due to sequential decoding, and non-autoregressive unified models suffer from weak generalization due to limited pretrained backbones. We introduce Muddit, a unified discrete diffusion transformer that enables fast and parallel generation across both text and image modalities. Unlike prior unified diffusion models trained from scratch, Muddit integrates strong visual priors from a pretrained text-to-image backbone with a lightweight text decoder, enabling flexible and high-quality multimodal generation under a unified architecture. Empirical results show that Muddit achieves competitive or superior performance compared to significantly larger autoregressive models in both quality and efficiency. The work highlights the potential of purely discrete diffusion, when equipped with strong visual priors, as a scalable and effective backbone for unified generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Muddit" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Muddit, a unified discrete diffusion transformer designed for efficient and flexible generation across text and image modalities. Muddit leverages a pre-trained text-to-image backbone to provide strong visual priors and incorporates a lightweight text decoder, enabling parallel generation across modalities. The authors highlight the limitations of autoregressive (AR) models (slow inference) and existing unified diffusion models (lack of strong pre-trained backbones). Muddit aims to overcome these limitations by using a MaskGIT-style discrete diffusion transformer. The paper presents empirical results demonstrating that Muddit achieves competitive or superior performance compared to larger AR models in terms of both quality and efficiency across tasks like text-to-image synthesis, image captioning, and visual question answering (VQA). The authors claim their work demonstrates the potential of purely discrete diffusion as a scalable and effective backbone for unified generation, offering a visual-first approach that contrasts with language-modeling-centric methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel combination of existing techniques. The core idea of using a discrete diffusion transformer for unified multimodal generation builds upon previous work like UniDisc and MaskGIT, but the key innovation is incorporating strong visual priors from a pre-trained text-to-image backbone (Meissonic).  The authors have essentially transferred the strengths of existing text-to-image diffusion models to the unified modeling setting. The visual-first approach is a counterpoint to the common trend of leveraging large language models. While the individual components (discrete diffusion, pre-trained backbone) are not entirely new, their combination and application to unified generation constitute a significant engineering contribution. It is worth mentioning that the authors did not fully address all related works, e.g. the "Autoregressive image generation without vector quantization" paper.

*   **Significance:** Muddit's significance lies in its potential for efficient and scalable multimodal generation. The parallel decoding of discrete diffusion transformers offers a significant speed advantage over AR models, which are known for slow inference. By successfully integrating strong visual priors, Muddit overcomes the limitations of previous unified diffusion models that were trained from scratch and often struggled with image quality. The good performance with only 1B parameters is also impressive and opens up possibilities for deploying such models on resource-constrained devices. However, its reliance on a pre-trained visual backbone also limits the model's capacity for rich text understanding and generation compared to LLM-based approaches.  The improvement over existing discrete diffusion models is significant. Moreover, the clear articulation of challenges associated with AR models in multimodal contexts contributes meaningfully to the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Efficient Inference:** Parallel decoding offers a substantial speed advantage over AR models.
    *   **Strong Visual Priors:** Leveraging a pre-trained text-to-image backbone significantly improves image quality.
    *   **Unified Architecture:**  The model successfully handles multiple tasks (T2I, I2T, VQA) within a single framework.
    *   **Competitive Performance:** Achieves good performance with a relatively small model size (1B parameters).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Pre-trained Backbone:**  Limits the model's capacity for complex text understanding and generation. The model may not be as suitable for tasks that demand deep linguistic reasoning.
    *   **Token-Level Discrete Representation:**  May lead to sub-optimal image quality compared to continuous diffusion models, especially at very high resolutions (though the paper reports good performance at 512x512).
    *   **Limited Novelty in Individual Components:**  While the overall architecture is novel, it mainly combines existing techniques.
    *   **Lack of ablation study:** A deeper ablation study on components like lightweight decoder/ the impact of different backbone would have been useful.

*   **Potential Influence:**  Muddit's success could inspire further research into discrete diffusion models for multimodal generation, particularly those that leverage pre-trained backbones. It highlights the potential of visual-first approaches as a complementary path to LLM-centric methods. The work may also encourage the development of more efficient inference techniques for diffusion models. As the visual-centric model is proposed, the community needs more effort to investigate the robustness, security (e.g. prompt injection attack), copyright and social impact of the proposed method.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

Muddit represents a valuable contribution to the field of multimodal generation. While it combines existing techniques, the resulting architecture is novel and addresses critical limitations of both AR models and existing unified diffusion models.  The demonstration of good performance and efficient inference with a relatively small model size is particularly significant.  However, the reliance on a pre-trained visual backbone and the token-level discrete representation limit the model's capabilities in some areas. Therefore, while influential, the technical advance does not warrant a higher rating at this time. Overall, the results clearly indicate a contribution to the field, but they are incremental and could benefit from more exploration into the theoretical insights of model performance.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Inference-time Scaling of Diffusion Models through Classical Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23614v1)
- **Authors**: Xiangcheng Zhang, Haowei Lin, Haotian Ye, James Zou, Jianzhu Ma, Yitao Liang, Yilun Du
- **Abstract**: Classical search algorithms have long underpinned modern artificial intelligence. In this work, we tackle the challenge of inference-time control in diffusion models -- adapting generated outputs to meet diverse test-time objectives -- using principles from classical search. We propose a general framework that orchestrates local and global search to efficiently navigate the generative space. It employs a theoretically grounded local search via annealed Langevin MCMC and performs compute-efficient global exploration using breadth-first and depth-first tree search. We evaluate our approach on a range of challenging domains, including planning, offline reinforcement learning, and image generation. Across all tasks, we observe significant gains in both performance and efficiency. These results show that classical search provides a principled and practical foundation for inference-time scaling in diffusion models. Project page at diffusion-inference-scaling.github.io.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a framework for scaling inference-time compute in diffusion models by leveraging classical search algorithms. It focuses on adapting generated outputs to meet diverse test-time objectives by orchestrating local and global search within the generative space. Local search is performed using annealed Langevin MCMC, while global exploration employs breadth-first search (BFS) and depth-first search (DFS) tree traversal. The approach is evaluated across planning, offline reinforcement learning, and image generation tasks, demonstrating performance and efficiency gains. The core idea is to use a verifier function to guide the search process, enabling diffusion models to adhere to constraints and achieve higher-quality samples more efficiently.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its systematic combination of classical search techniques with diffusion models. While using MCMC or guidance within diffusion models isn't entirely new, the explicit connection to BFS, DFS, and the theoretical grounding of local search as an instance of Langevin MCMC offers a fresh perspective. It formalizes inference-time scaling as a search problem, making explicit connections to established algorithms. However, the individual components (MCMC, BFS, DFS, guided diffusion) are not entirely novel themselves. The use of verifier functions is also not entirely novel but is presented in a way that scales compute effectively using traditional search methods.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in diffusion models: improving the quality and controllability of generated samples without extensive retraining. The demonstration that classical search can enhance both performance and efficiency is significant. It broadens the applicability of diffusion models to scenarios where satisfying specific constraints or optimizing for reward functions is critical (e.g., planning, robotics). The paper shows a way to scale existing diffusion models without incurring excessive computational costs. It has implications for making diffusion models more practical in resource-constrained environments.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Unified Framework:** The paper provides a general framework that encompasses both local and global search strategies, offering flexibility in how inference-time compute is allocated.
    *   **Theoretical Grounding:** The theoretical unification of training-free guidance with recurrence and Langevin MCMC provides valuable insights into the behavior of these techniques.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The extensive experiments across multiple domains (planning, RL, image generation) demonstrate the practical effectiveness of the approach.
    *   **Compute Efficiency:** The focus on compute efficiency is a key strength, as it makes the proposed methods more practical for real-world applications.
    *   **Adaptivity:** The DFS's adaptive compute allocation based on verifier feedback.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Hyperparameter Tuning:** The paper acknowledges the increased hyperparameter tuning requirements compared to training-based methods. This could limit the accessibility and ease of use of the proposed techniques.
    *   **Verifier Dependence:** The performance of the framework is heavily dependent on the quality of the verifier function. Designing effective verifiers for complex tasks may be challenging. It may not be applicable for tasks where no verifier exists.
    *   **Incremental Improvement**: While the gains achieved in various tasks are significant they do not signify a paradigm shift in the use of diffusion models, but rather showcase an incremental improvement.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's potential influence lies in its ability to make diffusion models more controllable, efficient, and widely applicable. It encourages further research into combining classical AI techniques with modern deep generative models. It could inspire development of automated hyperparameter tuning methods or adaptive verifier design techniques. The clear connection between search principles and diffusion model inference could foster a deeper understanding of these models and lead to new architectural innovations.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:** The paper presents a solid contribution by systematically exploring classical search for improved inference in diffusion models. While the individual components are not entirely new, their combination and the theoretical analysis offer valuable insights. The empirical results are convincing. The primary limitations are hyperparameter sensitivity and verifier dependency. Overall, this work offers a valuable and practical approach to scaling diffusion model inference and has significant potential to influence future research in this area, but it is not a revolutionary breakthrough.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Characterizing the Expressivity of Transformer Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23623v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaoda Li, Ryan Cotterell
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based language models (LMs) have achieved widespread empirical success, but their theoretical expressive power remains only partially understood. Prior work often relies on idealized models with assumptions -- such as arbitrary numerical precision and hard attention -- that diverge from real-world transformers. In this work, we provide an exact characterization of fixed-precision transformers with strict future masking and soft attention, an idealization that more closely mirrors practical implementations. We show that these models are precisely as expressive as a specific fragment of linear temporal logic that includes only a single temporal operator: the past operator. We further relate this logic to established classes in formal language theory, automata theory, and algebra, yielding a rich and unified theoretical framework for understanding transformer expressivity. Finally, we present empirical results that align closely with our theory: transformers trained on languages within their theoretical capacity generalize perfectly over lengths, while they consistently fail to generalize on languages beyond it.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper provides an exact characterization of the expressive power of fixed-precision transformer language models (LMs) with strict future masking and soft attention. It demonstrates that these models are precisely as expressive as a specific fragment of linear temporal logic (LTL) using only the past operator. This LTL fragment is further related to partially ordered deterministic finite automata (p.o. DFAs), R-trivial monoids and left-deterministic polynomials, offering a rich theoretical framework. The paper also includes empirical results that align with the theory: transformers trained on languages within their theoretical capacity generalize perfectly over lengths, while those trained on languages beyond their theoretical capacity consistently fail to generalize.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core contribution is the exact characterization of the expressivity of a specific, realistic transformer variant using LTL with only the past operator and demonstrating equivalence with p.o. DFAs, R-trivial monoids and left-deterministic polynomials. Previous work had provided upper bounds (C-RASP) or considered idealized models with hard attention. This precise characterization fills a gap in our understanding. Linking transformer expressivity to well-established algebraic and automata-theoretic concepts is also valuable.
*   **Significance:**  The paper provides a tighter and more accurate theoretical understanding of what fixed-precision transformers can represent. It clarifies the limitations of standard transformers, particularly regarding languages that require counting or context-free structures which are beyond their proven capabilities. The empirical results support the theoretical findings, highlighting that transformers perform according to their proven bounds. The findings help explain the behaviors of these systems, leading to a better way to work with their limitations, as well as inform on future architectures, that may or may not involve positional encodings. This result refines the theoretical toolkit for analyzing the power and limits of this important class of model. This may inform the design of future architectures or methods to augment existing transformers.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a *precise* and theoretically grounded characterization.
    *   The connection to established formal language theory, automata theory, and algebra is valuable.
    *   The empirical validation is rigorous and supports the theoretical claims.
    *   It addresses a gap in previous work by considering fixed precision and soft attention.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The analysis is limited to a specific type of transformer (fixed precision, strict future masking, soft attention). While this is more realistic than some idealizations, it still represents a simplification of complex, real-world models. The models do not learn positional encodings during the training. The scope could be considered a weakness.
    *   The paper's focus is primarily theoretical. While the empirical results are useful, they are relatively simple classification tasks. Future work could explore the practical implications of these findings in more complex NLP tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper offers a clear theoretical boundary on the capabilities of standard transformers. It may guide future research towards developing architectures or training methods to overcome these limitations. It also provides a framework for reasoning about the expressive power of different transformer variants.

Score: 7
Rationale: The paper provides a solid and original contribution to the theoretical understanding of transformers by showing precisely what is expressible using a given set of restrictions. The clear characterization of a specific class of fixed precision architectures with soft attention and strict masking makes it worthy of a higher score. The work adds to a body of knowledge that will help to better understand the capabilities, as well as the limitations of these systems. The theoretical results are clearly verified with consistent empirical behavior. Some weaknesses of the paper are that is that the theoretical model is still a simplification of real-world systems, and does not directly provide specific solutions to overcoming identified limitations. It does, however, provide a framework for reasoning and building better architectures.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ZeroSep: Separate Anything in Audio with Zero Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23625v1)
- **Authors**: Chao Huang, Yuesheng Ma, Junxuan Huang, Susan Liang, Yunlong Tang, Jing Bi, Wenqiang Liu, Nima Mesgarani, Chenliang Xu
- **Abstract**: Audio source separation is fundamental for machines to understand complex acoustic environments and underpins numerous audio applications. Current supervised deep learning approaches, while powerful, are limited by the need for extensive, task-specific labeled data and struggle to generalize to the immense variability and open-set nature of real-world acoustic scenes. Inspired by the success of generative foundation models, we investigate whether pre-trained text-guided audio diffusion models can overcome these limitations. We make a surprising discovery: zero-shot source separation can be achieved purely through a pre-trained text-guided audio diffusion model under the right configuration. Our method, named ZeroSep, works by inverting the mixed audio into the diffusion model's latent space and then using text conditioning to guide the denoising process to recover individual sources. Without any task-specific training or fine-tuning, ZeroSep repurposes the generative diffusion model for a discriminative separation task and inherently supports open-set scenarios through its rich textual priors. ZeroSep is compatible with a variety of pre-trained text-guided audio diffusion backbones and delivers strong separation performance on multiple separation benchmarks, surpassing even supervised methods.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ZeroSep, a novel training-free framework for audio source separation that leverages pre-trained text-guided audio diffusion models.  Instead of relying on task-specific training data, ZeroSep inverts mixed audio into the latent space of the diffusion model and then uses text prompts corresponding to individual sources to guide the denoising process, effectively isolating those sources.  The method demonstrates strong separation performance on multiple benchmarks, even surpassing supervised methods, and showcases versatility and open-set capability by handling diverse mixtures and textual queries.  The core idea is to repurpose a generative model for a discriminative task.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of achieving zero-shot audio source separation by repurposing text-guided diffusion models is the most compelling aspect of the work. It challenges the conventional wisdom that high-quality separation requires dedicated training.  While previous works have explored language-guided separation, they still required training on synthetic mixtures. This paper's training-free approach is a significant departure. However, the constituent techniques (diffusion models, DDIM/DDPM inversion, classifier-free guidance) are well-established. The novelty comes from the specific configuration and application of these existing tools to solve the source separation task in a training-free way. The critical balance of the guidance weight is a non-trivial find.

*   **Significance:** The implications of a successful training-free separation method are significant. It circumvents the need for massive labeled datasets, making separation more accessible and adaptable to diverse and open-set acoustic environments. The potential for generalization to unseen acoustic scenes is high. The observed correlation between the generative power of the base diffusion model and the separation performance suggests a promising path for future improvements, riding on advancements in audio generation. Additionally, it can spur the development of applications for audio editing, highlight, etc.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The concept of zero-shot source separation using pre-trained diffusion models is innovative.
    *   The framework is model-agnostic and can leverage different diffusion model backbones.
    *   It achieves competitive performance compared to supervised methods without any task-specific training.
    *   The paper provides thorough experimentation and analysis of key parameters like guidance weight and inversion methods.
    *   Clear demonstration of open-set capability handling a wider variety of real-world acoustic scenes.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies heavily on the availability and performance of pre-trained text-guided audio diffusion models. The results presented are fundamentally limited by the capabilities of the underlying models.
    *   While the results are promising, the separation performance is still limited in some cases and needs further work, as shown in the example failures shown in the supplemental information.
    *   The paper provides limited insight into cases where the method struggles, and further analysis would be useful. It would be beneficial to explore limitations regarding overlapping sounds or certain types of acoustic environments.
    *   The reliance on text-prompts is a potential limitation if the model lacks the knowledge for the sounds present in the mixture.
    *   The evaluation metrics may be biased to waveform-based methods. The perceptual fidelity of the separations must be improved.
    *   The ablation studies are helpful but could be more extensive (e.g. exploring the impact of prompt complexity/ambiguity).

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to shift the focus of audio source separation research towards leveraging pre-trained generative models. It could inspire new approaches for training-free or few-shot separation and open up new possibilities for applications in diverse acoustic environments.

* **Justification:** The work has a high degree of novelty and significance that has the potential to shift paradigm in the audio separation field. Given the quality of the work and potential future benefits, the following score is appropriate.

### AutoSchemaKG: Autonomous Knowledge Graph Construction through Dynamic Schema Induction from Web-Scale Corpora
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23628v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaxin Bai, Wei Fan, Qi Hu, Qing Zong, Chunyang Li, Hong Ting Tsang, Hongyu Luo, Yauwai Yim, Haoyu Huang, Xiao Zhou, Feng Qin, Tianshi Zheng, Xi Peng, Xin Yao, Huiwen Yang, Leijie Wu, Yi Ji, Gong Zhang, Renhai Chen, Yangqiu Song
- **Abstract**: We present AutoSchemaKG, a framework for fully autonomous knowledge graph construction that eliminates the need for predefined schemas. Our system leverages large language models to simultaneously extract knowledge triples and induce comprehensive schemas directly from text, modeling both entities and events while employing conceptualization to organize instances into semantic categories. Processing over 50 million documents, we construct ATLAS (Automated Triple Linking And Schema induction), a family of knowledge graphs with 900+ million nodes and 5.9 billion edges. This approach outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on multi-hop QA tasks and enhances LLM factuality. Notably, our schema induction achieves 95\% semantic alignment with human-crafted schemas with zero manual intervention, demonstrating that billion-scale knowledge graphs with dynamically induced schemas can effectively complement parametric knowledge in large language models.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AutoSchemaKG: Autonomous Knowledge Graph Construction through Dynamic Schema Induction from Web-Scale Corpora":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AutoSchemaKG, a framework for autonomously constructing knowledge graphs (KGs) from web-scale corpora without relying on predefined schemas.  The key innovation lies in dynamically inducing schemas directly from text by leveraging large language models (LLMs) to simultaneously extract knowledge triples (entities, events, and relationships) and conceptualize these into semantic categories. This approach models both entities and events, capturing temporal relationships and causality often missed in traditional entity-only KGs. The system processes over 50 million documents to create ATLAS, a KG family containing over 900 million nodes and 5.9 billion edges. The authors demonstrate that AutoSchemaKG outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on multi-hop question answering and enhances LLM factuality. Notably, the dynamically induced schema achieves a high degree of semantic alignment with human-crafted schemas without manual intervention.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strength:** The core idea of fully autonomous KG construction *without* predefined schemas is a significant departure from traditional approaches. While LLMs have been used in KG construction before, the simultaneous triple extraction and schema induction, particularly the emphasis on event modeling and conceptualization, represents a novel combination.
*   **Strength:**The emphasis on events and temporal relations distinguishes the approach from traditional entity-focused KGs. Capturing dynamic real-world knowledge significantly enhances its expressive power.
*   **Weakness:** The components – LLMs for triple extraction, conceptualization using LLMs – are individually well-established. The novelty resides in the *integration* and specific prompting/pipeline design. The novelty of the prompting engineering is not explored rigorously.

**Significance and Impact:**

*   **Strength:** The demonstrated scalability (billions of nodes/edges) is a significant achievement. Creating KGs of this size without manual schema engineering has the potential to democratize KG construction and enable applications in diverse domains.
*   **Strength:** The experimental results showing improvements in multi-hop QA and LLM factuality are compelling evidence of the effectiveness of the approach. The 95% semantic alignment with human-crafted schemas is impressive.
*   **Strength:** The framework's potential for cross-domain adaptability, eliminating the need for domain-specific customization, is a major advantage.
*   **Weakness:** While the paper shows improvements in downstream tasks, a deeper analysis of the types of queries/reasoning scenarios where AutoSchemaKG excels (compared to other KGs or retrieval methods) would be valuable. More qualitative evaluation of the schema produced and their impact would strengthen the claims.
*   **Weakness:** The ablations are fairly limited. How much does event extraction *specifically* contribute to the improved performance? How sensitive is the system to prompt engineering? What specific trade-offs were involved in the design of the prompts? The details surrounding the architecture and system configuration is limited.

**Overall Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of knowledge representation and reasoning. The framework addresses a critical limitation of traditional KG construction by eliminating the dependence on predefined schemas. The demonstrated scalability, performance improvements in downstream tasks, and cross-domain adaptability make AutoSchemaKG a promising approach for building large, dynamic, and adaptable knowledge resources. However, the paper does not explore the novelty of their prompting engineering, or system configuration, which weakens the arguments for overall impact. Future work should also focus on expanding the ablation studies.

### MCP Safety Training: Learning to Refuse Falsely Benign MCP Exploits using Improved Preference Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23634v1)
- **Authors**: John Halloran
- **Abstract**: The model context protocol (MCP) has been widely adapted as an open standard enabling the seamless integration of generative AI agents. However, recent work has shown the MCP is susceptible to retrieval-based "falsely benign" attacks (FBAs), allowing malicious system access and credential theft, but requiring that users download compromised files directly to their systems. Herein, we show that the threat model of MCP-based attacks is significantly broader than previously thought, i.e., attackers need only post malicious content online to deceive MCP agents into carrying out their attacks on unsuspecting victims' systems. To improve alignment guardrails against such attacks, we introduce a new MCP dataset of FBAs and (truly) benign samples to explore the effectiveness of direct preference optimization (DPO) for the refusal training of large language models (LLMs). While DPO improves model guardrails against such attacks, we show that the efficacy of refusal learning varies drastically depending on the model's original post-training alignment scheme--e.g., GRPO-based LLMs learn to refuse extremely poorly. Thus, to further improve FBA refusals, we introduce Retrieval Augmented Generation for Preference alignment (RAG-Pref), a novel preference alignment strategy based on RAG. We show that RAG-Pref significantly improves the ability of LLMs to refuse FBAs, particularly when combined with DPO alignment, thus drastically improving guardrails against MCP-based attacks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the security vulnerabilities of the Model Context Protocol (MCP), a widely adopted standard for integrating generative AI agents. It highlights a broadened threat model called "Total Retrieval-Agent Deception (TRADE)," where attackers only need to post malicious content online (Falsely Benign Attacks – FBAs) to compromise users' systems. The paper introduces a new dataset, MCP-FBAs, and explores using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and a novel Retrieval Augmented Generation for Preference alignment (RAG-Pref) method to improve LLMs' ability to refuse FBAs. The results show that while DPO can improve refusal, it varies significantly based on the model's pre-existing alignment, and RAG-Pref significantly enhances FBA refusal, particularly when combined with DPO. The paper introduces stricter metrics for evaluating LLM refusal in practical settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Positive:** The paper presents a novel threat model (TRADE) that expands the understanding of MCP vulnerabilities, specifically demonstrating attacks that don't require users to directly download compromised files. The introduction of the MCP-FBAs dataset is also a valuable contribution, filling a gap in available resources for studying and mitigating MCP-related attacks. The RAG-Pref method appears to be a novel approach to preference alignment using retrieval, offering a training-free way to improve FBA refusal. The more stringent LLM refusal metrics are also novel.
*   **Negative:** While the TRADE attack itself is new, retrieval-based attacks and the general concept of "falsely benign" prompts are not entirely new in the realm of LLM security. The use of RAG, while adapted for preference alignment, builds upon existing techniques.

**Significance:**

*   **Positive:** Given the increasing adoption of MCP, the paper addresses a crucial and timely security concern. Successfully demonstrating TRADE attacks against Claude 3.7 underscores the real-world implications of these vulnerabilities. The findings regarding the limitations of DPO and the effectiveness of RAG-Pref provide valuable insights for improving the security of MCP-based systems. The stricter metrics are useful for properly evaluating LLM/agent safety.
*   **Negative:** While the improvements shown by RAG-Pref are significant, the absolute refusal rates achieved by the LLMs in the experimental evaluation sections are often still not overwhelmingly high. There is more progress needed in improving general LLM security and mitigating these attacks. The study primarily focuses on the FileSystem server and may not be generalizable to all MCP servers. The empirical analysis can be further strengthened with a more diverse set of models and attack scenarios.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear problem definition and threat model.
*   Introduction of a new, high-quality dataset.
*   Demonstration of a practical attack exploiting MCP vulnerabilities.
*   Rigorous evaluation of DPO and RAG-Pref with detailed metrics.
*   Novel training-free method for improving preference alignment (RAG-Pref).

**Weaknesses:**

*   The absolute refusal rates achieved are not universally very high, implying more research is necessary.
*   Focus primarily on one MCP server (FileSystem server) for experimental validation.
*   Limited generalizability due to specific tool chains being targeted in the attack vectors.
*   No discussion of additional safeguards such as sandboxing, which might further mitigate the potential impact of these attacks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the growing field of LLM security, particularly within the context of the rapidly evolving MCP ecosystem. The TRADE attack and associated dataset are significant, demonstrating novel vulnerabilities. While the individual components are not entirely groundbreaking, their combination, particularly the use of RAG-Pref for preference alignment, provides a promising approach to mitigate MCP-related attacks. The findings have immediate practical implications for developers and organizations deploying MCP-based systems. However, the limitations mentioned above prevent a higher score.

### Are Reasoning Models More Prone to Hallucination?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23646v1)
- **Authors**: Zijun Yao, Yantao Liu, Yanxu Chen, Jianhui Chen, Junfeng Fang, Lei Hou, Juanzi Li, Tat-Seng Chua
- **Abstract**: Recently evolved large reasoning models (LRMs) show powerful performance in solving complex tasks with long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning capability. As these LRMs are mostly developed by post-training on formal reasoning tasks, whether they generalize the reasoning capability to help reduce hallucination in fact-seeking tasks remains unclear and debated. For instance, DeepSeek-R1 reports increased performance on SimpleQA, a fact-seeking benchmark, while OpenAI-o3 observes even severer hallucination. This discrepancy naturally raises the following research question: Are reasoning models more prone to hallucination? This paper addresses the question from three perspectives. (1) We first conduct a holistic evaluation for the hallucination in LRMs. Our analysis reveals that LRMs undergo a full post-training pipeline with cold start supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and verifiable reward RL generally alleviate their hallucination. In contrast, both distillation alone and RL training without cold start fine-tuning introduce more nuanced hallucinations. (2) To explore why different post-training pipelines alters the impact on hallucination in LRMs, we conduct behavior analysis. We characterize two critical cognitive behaviors that directly affect the factuality of a LRM: Flaw Repetition, where the surface-level reasoning attempts repeatedly follow the same underlying flawed logic, and Think-Answer Mismatch, where the final answer fails to faithfully match the previous CoT process. (3) Further, we investigate the mechanism behind the hallucination of LRMs from the perspective of model uncertainty. We find that increased hallucination of LRMs is usually associated with the misalignment between model uncertainty and factual accuracy. Our work provides an initial understanding of the hallucination in LRMs.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary, critical evaluation, and score for the paper "Are Reasoning Models More Prone to Hallucination?"

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether large reasoning models (LRMs), designed for chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, are more susceptible to hallucination (generating factually incorrect information) compared to their non-reasoning counterparts.  The authors perform a systematic evaluation of various LRMs across fact-seeking tasks, analyzing the impact of different post-training pipelines (SFT+RL, RL-only, and SFT-only) on factual accuracy. They identify two cognitive behaviors associated with increased hallucination (Flaw Repetition and Think-Answer Mismatch) and explore the connection between model uncertainty and factuality. They find that SFT+RL models are less prone to hallucination, while RL-only and SFT-only pipelines often lead to increased factual errors.  They also show that model miscalibration correlates with hallucination and explore parameter updates as potential factors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a critical and timely question in the field of large language models: the relationship between reasoning abilities and factual accuracy. While the individual components (evaluating factuality, analyzing post-training methods, examining uncertainty) have been explored separately, the paper's novelty lies in bringing these aspects together to specifically address *hallucinations in reasoning models* and connecting them to distinct post-training methodologies. Connecting the training methods with the cognitive failures of flawed repetition and think-answer mismatch increases the novelty of the work.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for the development and deployment of LRMs. The paper demonstrates that simply enhancing reasoning abilities does not guarantee improved factual accuracy, and in some cases, can even worsen it. Understanding the nuances of different post-training pipelines is crucial for building more reliable LRMs, particularly for high-stakes applications where factual correctness is paramount. Identifying cognitive behaviours linked to hallucination opens avenues for novel interventions. The analysis of the parameter shift in relation to the model output is a solid start, yet is also one of the shortcomings of this paper.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough empirical evaluation, covering a range of LRMs and post-training pipelines.
    *   **Behavioral Analysis:** The identification of Flaw Repetition and Think-Answer Mismatch as key cognitive behaviors contributing to hallucination is valuable.
    *   **Mechanistic Investigation:** Exploring the connection between model uncertainty and factuality provides insights into the inner workings of LRMs and the causes of hallucination.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the experimental setup, results, and analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Control:** The study relies on publicly released models, limiting control over confounding factors such as pretraining data and training schedules. This is noted by the authors.
    *   **LLM-as-a-Judge Subjectivity:** The reliance on LLM-as-a-Judge for evaluating factual correctness introduces a degree of subjectivity, although the authors attempt to mitigate this by using a state-of-the-art reasoning LLM as the judge. This is also noted by the authors.
    *   **Parameter Analysis Limitation:** While the initial parameter analysis is a welcome start, it doesn't fully explain the phenomenon and indicates that further research is warranted. It acknowledges that parameter change cannot fully explain the phenomenon.
    *   **Scope of Hallucination:** The study focuses on short-form QA tasks, which may not fully generalize to more complex generation scenarios. The study could also discuss more the connection to prompt engineering and the types of tasks/prompts that give rise to these hallucinations, and discuss how to mitigate the mistakes.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on LRM development, encouraging a greater focus on factuality alongside reasoning abilities. The identified cognitive behaviors and the uncertainty-based analysis could inspire new techniques for mitigating hallucination. This paper serves as a good example of how the field should be conducting evaluations to prevent future problems down the road.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by systematically investigating the relationship between reasoning abilities and hallucination in LRMs. The thorough evaluation, behavioral analysis, and mechanistic investigation provide important insights for the development of more reliable LRMs. While the study is limited by its reliance on publicly released models and the use of LLM-as-a-Judge, the findings are robust enough to warrant a score of 7. Its focus on training methodology and cognitive flaws elevates it beyond a mere empirical survey. It also presents some limitations that is acknowledged and the potential for improvement with future work. It could be improved if it was more general in scope, and had more findings that could be easily generalized or adopted for other use cases.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Continuous Chain of Thought Enables Parallel Exploration and Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23648v1)
- **Authors**: Halil Alperen Gozeten, M. Emrullah Ildiz, Xuechen Zhang, Hrayr Harutyunyan, Ankit Singh Rawat, Samet Oymak
- **Abstract**: Current language models generate chain-of-thought traces by autoregressively sampling tokens from a finite vocabulary. While this discrete sampling has achieved remarkable success, conducting chain-of-thought with continuously-valued tokens (CoT2) offers a richer and more expressive alternative. Our work examines the benefits of CoT2 through logical reasoning tasks that inherently require search capabilities and provide optimization and exploration methods for CoT2. Theoretically, we show that CoT2 allows the model to track multiple traces in parallel and quantify its benefits for inference efficiency. Notably, one layer transformer equipped with CoT2 can provably solve the combinatorial "subset sum problem" given sufficient embedding dimension. These insights lead to a novel and effective supervision strategy where we match the softmax outputs to the empirical token distributions of a set of target traces. Complementing this, we introduce sampling strategies that unlock policy optimization and self-improvement for CoT2. Our first strategy samples and composes $K$ discrete tokens at each decoding step to control the level of parallelism, and reduces to standard CoT when $K=1$. Our second strategy relies on continuous exploration over the probability simplex. Experiments confirm that policy optimization with CoT2 indeed improves the performance of the model beyond its initial discrete or continuous supervision.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Continuous Chain-of-Thought (CoT2), a method for enabling parallel exploration and reasoning in language models. Instead of discrete token sampling, CoT2 uses continuously-valued tokens, allowing models to track multiple reasoning traces simultaneously. The authors provide a theoretical analysis showing the benefits of CoT2, demonstrating its ability to solve problems like the Minimum Non-Negative Sum (MNNS) task using a single-layer transformer.  They introduce a novel supervised training strategy (CSFT) that matches softmax outputs to the empirical token distributions of target traces, and policy optimization techniques for self-improvement. Experiments on MNNS, ProntoQA, and ProsQA tasks demonstrate that policy optimization with CoT2 improves performance beyond initial supervision. The paper further offers a theoretical analysis of base CoT2, CoT2-MTS and discrete CoT.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using continuous tokens for chain-of-thought reasoning has some precedence in prior work like COCONUT, CODI, and CCOT.  However, this paper introduces distinct algorithmic approaches, most notably the supervision method explicitly targets implicit parallelism, as well as their methods of sampling to control the level of parallelism during decoding (CoT2-MTS) and a purely continuous method over the probability simplex. The investigation and formalization of the benefits of parallel exploration are also noteworthy. The study of tradeoffs between accuracy and the embedding dimension is valuable.

*   **Significance:** The ability to track multiple reasoning paths in parallel is a significant advantage, particularly for tasks requiring search or complex reasoning. The theoretical guarantees provided offer a solid foundation for the approach.  The empirical results, while promising, are currently limited to specific tasks (MNNS, ProntoQA, ProsQA). These tasks are carefully chosen to be amenable to parallelization but might not be representative of all reasoning problems. The reported improvements, while statistically significant, could be more substantial to truly revolutionize CoT. Further, practical large-scale demonstrations are missing. The findings showing a need for increased embedding dimension also present a hurdle in terms of computational efficiency and applicability for larger models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Solid theoretical grounding and analysis
    *   Introduction of novel supervision and sampling strategies.
    *   Clear articulation of the benefits of parallel reasoning.
    *   Careful selection of tasks that theoretically benefit from CoT2.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited empirical evaluation to specific tasks.
    *   Lack of demonstration on standard benchmarks used for evaluating CoT prompting on Language Models.
    *   Increased embedding dimension requirements can be a barrier to deployment.
    *   The practical gains on the benchmark tasks presented might not revolutionize current CoT prompting.
    *   While the sampling method (CoT2-MTS) attempts to bridge the continuous and discrete, an ideal approach might focus more on a fully continuous representation instead.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research on efficient reasoning in language models, particularly methods that explore multiple hypotheses simultaneously. The theoretical analysis and novel supervision strategy could provide inspiration for future work. Further work is needed to assess its applicability to more general tasks and large language models.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper presents a theoretically sound and novel approach to chain-of-thought reasoning with continuous tokens, it needs more substantial empirical validation on standard tasks and scaling experiments. The theoretical contribution and insight into the benefits of parallel reasoning are the paper's main strengths. The limited empirical evaluation, as well as the dependence on increased embedding dimension, are weaknesses that limit the score. The work builds upon previous work on continous COT, thus a lower score is warranted.
